===== 0002601_19150911_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Peripatetic Preachers. On Tuesday evening the Bold-street Wes- leyans formally welcomed their new circuit minister, the Rev. Percy meson. I might be misunderstood were I to draw even for the purpose of illustration upon the case of the nameless one whose philosophy, whatever it may be is supposed to be instilled into the minds of his pupils while he is "going to and fro in the earth and walking up and down in it " ; and yet that of Aristotle, whose habit it was while teaching to walk up and down the Lyceum at Athens, somehow hardly semi to fit. At all events, I have no misgiving re- specting my headline. Mr. meson is one of several hundreds of brethren of his denomina- tion who during the last two or three weeks have been "walking .up and down" bidding farewell to those who "for the space of three years" haves been their charges in matters religious, and then betaking themselves to fresh woods and pastures new. It is indeed a wandering life, thus triennially, and some- times oftener, to repack one's belongings and hie away to an altogether different environ- ment. A young lady who desired •to make this life her own by linking her fortunes with those of "a Methodist itinerant who keeps for ever moving," was told by her mother on her wedding morn, "I always said you would do for a gipsy, and now you have taken the nearest thing you could get to it." Consoling. _ _

Tuesday's gathering at Bold-street was publicly to recognise a fresh 'appointment, and, as I have observed, the new minister had just taken leave of old and tried friends and now found himself in the midst of others concern- ing whose qualities he as yet knows little or nothing experimentally. After all, laughter is not remote from tears, and if the situation has its pathos for the " travelling " preacher, it has also its humorous side. The story may not be new to readers, but one could give the name of the minister who, in circumstances similar to those in which Mr. Meson has lately been placed, called to see an old member who was said to be feeling keenly his impending departure. "Still, you know," said he, hop- ing to console her, "the Lord never sends a servant away without sending a better one to fill his place." " Ah," sighed the old dame; that's what they told me when you came, and I know better!" Ministers' Wives.

These, too, come in for a good deal of critic- ism periodically. When a steward is on the look-out for a minister, he is careful to ask also about the lady of the manse. I know of one such official who had no fewer than a dozen questions with regard to the minister's wife—" Is she so-and-so or such-and-such? Can she do this and that?" So they ran, laymen being apt to forget that the great pro- portion of these ladies are laymen's daugh- ters, and that the home training and domestic habits with which some of them are so ready to find fault are the outcome of the life of the layman's home. In the particular in- .stance now referred to, the minister simply replied: " The angelic creature your circuit requires in the minister's wife is neither in the heaven above, nor in the earth beneath, nor in the waters under the earth!" The Stewards.

I had intended to pass forthwith to quite a different subject, but perhaw if I left it there misapprehension might arise in some quarters. When all is said and done from the point just fouched,one is bound to writewith lavish praise of these same Methodist stewards and their good wives. As I have myself heard a Wes- leyan minister gdeclare (and the sentiment again found expression in Warrington during the week-end), no church can have more loyal and unselfish officers; and this has been a busy and harassing time with many of them. The ladies have overhauled everything inside the house, and the gentlemen everything outside, not forgetting the drains, which a sanitary expert must certify to be in perfect order. The generous-hearted stewards send sundry tradesmen's boys up to the manse with good things fqi the larder, while the coal 'cellar is not always left a " dark, aching void." Methodism and Methodist preachers owe much to their circuit stewards, and may the rich• traditions of their office never deteriorate in coming years. " Art for Every Day."

Last evening the Head Master of the School of Art lectured on this topic, and the title set me thinking of the time when Millais was so acandalised by the discovery • that his " Bubbles" (a copy of which, by-the-way, is before me as I write) was to be used as a soap advertisement, although he had been paid £2,000 for it. How the man in the street has advanced in his appreciation of art since that period! The hoardings of the Metropolis and other great cities nowadays bear comparison with a high-class picture gallery, and the same may be said of many railway carriages and some of the larger railway stations; and it is reasonable to claim that this art appeals to a larger and- more varied public than the art gallery of the orthodox type. As a brother scribe remarked years ago, the pave- ment artist is being displaced by the poster artist. Every railway ciimpany tempts you away for a holiday with scenes of rural beauty and content; each steamship company appeals to your pocket with pictures of graceful ships on an azure sea; every theatre invites you to its box office with a fine painting of its latest success. For this, among many , reasons, there is greater need of an _Art School in our midst than ever there was. May it flourish exceedingly, and may Mr. Mawson have his -due reward in its prosperity. Stale Bread, eh? - - - •

[like that story of the 34 years' old wheat which was reproduced in last week's " Ex- aminer." At the same time perhaps it is possible to beat it in a sense. I know of a place in Derbyshire--Ambaston, in fact— where there is a loaf of bread 600 years old, When King John was on the throne, it was included in a grant of land from the Crown, and to this day it continues to be prized by the Soar family. But even that may be re- garded as modern bread as compared with an Assyrian loaf which a French explorer found a few years ago. Bun-shaped, it was wrapped in a cloth in a tightly-sealed sarcophagus' and was in perfectly good condition, although sup- posed to have been baked in 560 B.C. At Pom- peii about the same time there was unearthed an oven containing charred loaves' every one of which bore the name of the baker, the lettering being still quite legible.

4' Modern \ldolatry." " Marjorie's " last week's contribution began with &paragraph respecting the modern craze for amulets and mascots. When I was a youngster mysterious meanings were often attached to quite ordinary incidents. Let the teapot lid be forgotten for a moment when tea was brewed; then assuredly someone would drop in to share the meal. Let one seated at the table receive two spoons with her cup by mistake; it was, a: sign that she would he married within the ensuing twelve months. If she found - a leaf floating in her tea, it meant that she had an admirer. She must immediately stir the contents quickly, and then hold the spoon upright in the .middle of the cup. If the tea-leaf was attracted to it, the swain would be certain to call that very (lay ; otherwise he would present himself some- time later. Even in this twentieth century in some parts of the country in which I have lived, a floating tea-leaf is considered "a sure sign of a visitor." The leaf is removed, placed on the back of the left hand, and struck with the lower side of the right fist. This is re- peated until the wet leaf is transferred from the one to the other, and as the operator has at each stroke repeated successively the days of the week, the adhesion to the right hand fixes the date of the projected visit. It would be easy to multiply examples, but readers •generally will be familiar with many of them. i

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 11, 1915.

him. He would never do it again. "You know how you feel," he added, "when you have been done." (Laughter.)

Under The Kaiser.

After a few humorous allusions to the Kaisees claims to culture, the speaker went on to say that there were a few people, not yet certified, who thought they could live as well under the Kaiser as under King George V. "As I have said before," said Mr. Crooks, "I would rather see every man, woman and child belonging to Great Britain blotted off the face of the earth than living under the Kaiser." "I have been twice round the British Empire," Mr. Crooks said in referring to whit he described as the British family spirit. "Not at my own expense," he added, amidst laughter. "I thought I had better tell you that, in case you found it out. (Renewed laughter.) The last time 1 went at the in- vitation of the Commonwealth of Australia, who invited twenty legislators—not common or garden M.P.'s. Those selected were eight Liberals, eight Tories, three lords—and me. (Laughter.) Wherever we went, the most mar- vellous thing of all was that the farther we got away from Great Britain, the nearer we got to home. The one cry we met with was: 'Tell us about home.'"

Having alluded to the popularity of the hymn "Abide With Me," in the Colonies, Mr. Crooks stated that that hymn was, in ninety-nine cases out of a hundred, selected when music was suggested. There was no talk of blood and power, but an everlasting belief in a Great Creator, who loved and cared for us all.

and should have been a dirty race if we had not gone in to help them; but to-day we were fighting for greater things. Hate Conscription !

His last word was this: Lord Kitchener did not authorise them to talk about compulsion. And he. (the -speaker) hated Conscription ,lock, stock and barrel—not politically (for politics were dead), but for Britain, even if it suited every other country. Because, he did not want the neutral countries of the world to say that the British boy had to be pulled out by the scruff of the neck to do the duty his loyalty should have demanded. Fifteen months ago we had 250,000 of the best fighters and best swearers (they kept the Recording Angel busy night and day in the canteen! but they were fine men for all that), and now we had turned our Army into a volunteer force of practically three and a half million men. Let us remember the triumphs of the Volun- tary System! There was no better-behaved or better-treated soldier than the British "Tommy." (Applause.) Via Buttermarket-street and Sankey--street the procession then returned to the Town Hall.

"THE QUICK, ALARMING DRUM."

RECRUITING CAMPAIGN TO BRING IN TERRITORIALS.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
Voluntaryism Generally Advocated.

MR. WILL CROOKS INVITES ALL TO JOIN IN A

"FAMILY JOB."

During the present week Warrington has been experiencing the second of Lord Derby's re- cruiting campaigns, in which everything is done with the thoroughness and earnestness that have characterised all his Lordship's movements since the war began. The appeal has been made for recruits in general, but for the Territorials in particular.

Three bands have been engaged, and "the quick, alarming drum" has called the attention of all and sundry in town and district, to the need for answering the call. These bands are _Lord Derby's recruiting band (under the con- ductorship of Mr. A. E. Halford), the band of the Second South Lancashire Regiment (under Mr. R. E. Cahill), and the pipers of the Liver- pool Scottish. The decorated and illuminated tramcar, with its wounded soldier passengers, has played a prominent part in the appeal.

The arrangements locally have been in the hands of Captains Mathams and Eric Fair- dough, with the assistance of Messrs. E. A. Sanders and H. Hayes, the local political agents, as hon. secretaries. Captain Townroe represents the West Lancashire Territorial Association.

By kind permission of the Mayor and Corpora- tion, Lord Derby's recruiting band will con- clude the week's recruiting music at a special festiVal in the Bank Park to-morrow, Septem- ber 12th. In full force, the Liverpool Scottish Pipers will also be there. The Mayor will deliver a final address.

authorities hoped to get a large number of re- cruits for the gallant British Army, for although Warrington had done fairly well she could still do much better. He did not think that the young men refrained from enlisting because they were unpatriotic, but rather because they did not realise the importance of the war. Germany's great aim was to get at Great Britain. Let the people of this country think of it. In the light of what Germany had done in the past, he wondered if the, people of this country would like the Germans to come into this little island? "For myself," the speaker continued, "I love this country better than my own life. If Eng- land went under, everything that made life worth living would go under with her. When we decided to, participate in this war we entered upon a tremendous job. People thought it . would be a simple thing to crush Germany, but we found that in spite of the gallant re- sistance offered by little Belgium, the fine courage shown by the Serbians, and the help given by the French and the Russians, Ger- many was still in possession of a very large portion of France. All that must ,be won back, and also the land which the Russians have lost." He had no doubt, added the speaker, that if England won a lasting peace would be arranged. .

===== 0002601_19150911_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
TO PATRIOTISM AND CONSCIENCE.

Mayor And Member Make Forceful

Appeals.

The first of the series of meetings was held out- side the Town Hall at noon on Monday, and, despite the inconvenient hour, was well attended, though mostly by ladies. The band of the 2nd South Lancashires were drawn up in front of the steps and played a spirited air or two—not forgetting a topical and popular theme occasionally—whilst the listeners gath- ered. A cheer was raised when the Mayor, wear- ing his robes of office, emerged from ,the Town Hall doors, and it was repeated when it was seen that he was accompanied by the Borough Member, who had paid a flying visit to the town in order to take part in the recruiting campaign. The Member, was wearing his naval uniform, and he acknowledged with a smart salute the cheers of the crowd as well as the greetings of the military officers assembled on the steps. The meeting was a brief one, for there was much work to be crowded into the dinner hour.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
MUCH DONE, BUT MORE TO DO.

The Mayor, in a few prefatory words, wished every success to the recruiting campaign, and went on to opine, amid cheers, that Warrington had good reason to be proud of the boys of the 4th South Lancashires. He sincerely trusted that the appeal which was being made that week would not fall on deaf ears. He did not overlook the fact that Warrington rendered much good service in the matter of the pro- duction of munitions of war, but he was also convinced of the fact that there were in the town a: large number of young pen who ought to be doing their duty with the brave Terri- torials. (Hear, hear.) If a young man could not conscientiously say that he was engaged in the production of munitions his duty was quite clear! It was to put on khaki, or the blue naval uniform, and play his part, that way, in seeing the war through. (Hear, hear.) Every fit young man who was not making munitions and was not in the Army or Navy was nothing but a slacker. "And," added his Worship, "I am ashamed of anyone who comes within that category."

COME FORWARD, YOUNG MAN-!

When the cheers which greeted the Mayor's brief speech had died away, the Borough Member briefly addressed the assemblage, re- marking that he was glad that his Worship had afforded him the opportunity and the privilege of appealing to the young men. of Warrington to come forward and take the places of the young men of the town who had, alas! fallen in their country's glorious cause. Warrington, nay, his country, appealed to every young man, provided he had no legitimate reason for not doing so, to join the forces of the Crown, and to lose no time about it either! (Hear, hear.) "Come for- ward, younr, man, at this hour of your country's need!" the Member continued—" come forward and take your place among the heroes that War- rington has already sent to the war! I believe there is no town in England that has more legitimate cause for prid,e in its gallant Terri- torial Regiment than has Warrington. (Cheers.) Let not only the memory of that regiment remain with us, but let the regiment, in being, remain with us, and that can only happen if the town and the locality sends out fresh men to take the places of the brave lads who have given their lives. This week's cam- paign has been organised not with the idea of driving into the Army men who ought not to go; indeed, it has not been 'prganised with the idea of driving young men who really ought to go. No, the idea is to make an appeal to the young men—to appeal to their patriotism, to their chivalry, to their better instincts; aye, to their very consciences! Mr. Smith went on t.o ask "How any young man, in the tragedy through which we are plissing, who is able 10 go but who does not,,ean meet the heroes when they come back, can hear their stories, not of their own heroism but of their comrades' valour, and have to keep silent be- cause he has nothing to say of the part that he played in the war? I cannot believe that there are many such men in this patriotic town, and this campaign is promoted in the hope that at the end of this week there will be no such men." (Applause.) Mr. Smith finished by ex- pressing the hope that the response to the appeal would be so great that the gallant regiment which Warrington delighted to honour would be able to add lustre to its name and numbers to its ranks to continue the splendid fight for the honour of Britain. (Loud applause.) THANKING THE MAYOR.

. Captain Townroe proposed a vote of thanks to the Mayor, who, he said, was unfailing in his efforts for the welfare of Warrington and of the nation. (Applause.) They also hoped that the regular forces of the Crown, represented there by Colonel MOcatta, would receive more men from the town, but they were somewhat prejudiced in favour of their own gallant regi- ment—the 4th South Lancashires. (Applause.) If that regiment was to continue to exist and not be fused with other battalions, more men must be obtained from Warrington and district. Three weeks ago the bands and speakers arrived in Bolton on Monday morning, and by Saturday night they had obtained 600 recruits for the local battalion. (Applause.) Captain Townroe also acknowledged the interest shown in the campaign by Colonel Moggridge, the officer commanding the depot, Orford Barracks. The bands and speakers then left for meet- ings near some of the local works.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
HOPE OF THE MILITARY

AUTHORITIES.

The third meeting of the campaign was held at noon on Monday near the Dallam Forge and Walker's .Brewery. Lord Derby's Band was in attendance and played several popular airs whilst the crowd were waiting for the men to come from their work. . The first speaker was Alderman Bennett, who said that he had addressed ..any meetings in Warrington, but few were Of such importance as that recruiting tmeeting. The military

===== 0002601_19150911_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
Country Must Fight Hard.

In order to win, however, this country would have to fight hard, for Germany would stop at nothing in order to accomplish the task which she had set out to accomplish. He was glad to hear that Messrs. Pearson • and Knowles were erecting ,a new plant for the purpose of making munitiens of war, and to the men engaged on such war work he did not make his appeal for service, for they were already helping their .country. He was confident, however, that there were still a large number of Men- who were not engaged in the production of muni- tions of any sort and who could be well spared from their present occupations. His hearers had, doubtlessly, all seen " Tommies " who had been to the Front, and had come back, wounded yet smiling. How the young men could look upon them and still resist the call he did not know. If the young men did not join up pretty quickly they would, he felt assured, have cause to regret it. Warrington was able to boast of a V.C. hero— Corporal Tombs--who had recently been amongst them and had been admired by them all. (Cheers.) It was possible for each young man in his audience td do some signal service for his country and his fellows, and thus emu- late the example of the brave Corporal. In conclusion, Alderman Bennett referred to the local Volunteer Corps, and said it was not half as strong as it ought to be. He appealed most earnestly to all eligible men over forty years of age to join the corps—a section of a body which had been recognised by the King. and which could help in many ways. (Hear, hear.) THE MEN WHO DON'T WAKE UP!

Mr. E. J. Coleman said there were still many men in Warrington who were eligible to join some branch of his Majesty's forces, but who had not yet'clone so. Before the war Belgium was one of the most beautiful countries in the world, but to-day she was little more than a cemetery. The Allies were not yet on German territory; they had not yet overcome the Germans. Still there were men who did not yet 'realise that they were wanted. If the women and the older people would only carry the message and em- phasise its paramount importance, the able- bodied men would come in, once they realised that they were fighting for the very existence of the country, and that it is still possible that she might be overcome. (Hear, hear.) Bolton had sent many thousands of men, and lately, in eight days, they had recruited a further 825. Let Warrington take that as an object-lesson. (Hear, hear.) If this country could not get the necessary recruits under the voluntary system, they would have to get them some other way. Mr. Asquith had said that the country was spending money on the wart at the rate of three million pounds per day. War Casting £2,000 A Minute.

That amounted to ..t2,000 per minute. Some- thing to think about, wasn't it? People en- gaged on Government work were earning good money, and were spending it, in many cases, foolishly. To such people he would say: "Put a bit away for a rainy day !" for that day would surqy come. The country was living at present on its capital, and the capital would all have to be put back after the war. The British Colonies had responded nobly, to the call of the Motherland. Mr. S. Thompson said this country was fight- ing, with its back to the wall, against the most barbarous foe with which they had ever had to contend. They were fighting against a nation that had not the slightest respect for law', human or divine, and whose every word was An appeal would be made specially that week for men to join the 4th South Lan- cashire Regiment—a regiment which had al- ways borne itself bravely. Recruits, however, would also be taken for any other regiment— Territorial or Regular. The people of Britain were all desirous of seeing this war through to the 'end, and of making a good clean job of it. They .did not want Conscription, for Germany would then be able to say: "Look at Great Britain, who boasted of her freedom, and yet cannot find sufficient of its men to fight—and die, if need be—for her liberty in her time of trial." Sergeant Ralph Done also spoke.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
THE KAISER'S HEALTH.

Will Crooks Visits Warrington

And Confesses.

THE BRITON AND HOME.

The visit of Mr. Will Crooks, M.P. for Woolwich, to the town on Monday evening, drew a large crowd to Bridge Foot, where a good 'meeting was held. Mr. C. E. Parker, who presided, briefly intro- duced the speaker, making allusion to Mr. Crooks's recent visit to the Front. Having been out there, Mr. Crooks could give them a true idea of what it was like. Mr. Crooks needed no introduction to an English audience. He (the Chairman) might as well speak of the virtues of the Prime Minister as those of Mr. Crooks. Mr. Will Crooks, who is well known as a speaker who invariably "gets there" with the crowd by reason of the homeliness and broad humour, the humanness and the graphic quality' of his fashion of oratory, was in good fettle, and held the attention and interest 'of his audience throughout his address, which he began by saying that the present war was a family war. A Family War.

"This is a family war," he said, "and we are having a family talk about it. The family is in trouble, and we have lined up four square as Lancashire men always do. The war began in Germany, and it has got to finish there. (Applause.) We have never been quite sure how much of this beautiful, wonderful, marvel- lous Kaiser we are responsible for. (Laughter.) We praised him, we lauded him, we sent depu- tations to him. I have been four timss to Germany with a message from Britain to the Kaiser to this effect: Would you be so good as to remember, if ever we get into trouble, that the smite blood runs through our veins as through yours? And William said, 'Certainly, we will think it over!' We are fighting now for right and justice, and we are going to get it, too." (Applause.) In 1908, Mr. Crooks continued, he went to Cologne, and at a banquet, proposed the health of the Kaiser, for which might God forgive

===== 0002601_19150911_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
A Family Job.

We were all in this war. Was there a man or woman who dared to say nowadays that this was a Government war or a capitalist war? "By the living God," he affirmed, "it is a people's war, and it is for the liberty of the people that we are fighting." (Applause.) After referring to the response which had been made by all classes to the call to the Colours. Mr,. Crooks asked those in the audience who had no relatives serving with the Colours to hold up their hands. None did so. "Now," said Mr. Crooks, "hands up those who have relatives with the Forces," And apparently the whole audience put its hands up, whereat Mr. Crooks inquired: "Who can say now that it is not a family job ?" A Fight For Liberty.

"I had to turn away and wipe my eyes," the speaker said, describing an interview with a wounded soldier, on the occasion of his visit to the Front. "I thought to myself, 'What have I done that he should give life or limb for me?' We talk about the sacrifices we are making, but if we gave all we possess or are likely to possess, we should never pay those men for what they have s. done for us._ (Ap- plause.) Every one of them deserves the Victoria Cross. (Applause.) "Sometimes," Mr. Crooks went on, "I ask myself why it is that some of our people hold liberty so cheap. I will tell you. Liberty is cheap for some men, because they have never fought for it. They have forgotten that they enjoy liberties for which their fathers or grand- fathers died on the scaffold, in penal servitude, or on many a bloody battlefield. Sometimes people say, 'I have no money nor land; how can Ibe a partner in the Empire?' And' yet they possess that which money cannot buy—the right to say their soul is their own." (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150911_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMAN CONFIDENCE.

Mr. F. L. Joseph, of Liverpool, in the course of a short address, said one thing that .every one must chi to-day , was to give his unquestion- ing support and his unswerving loyalty to the Government. The time for idle bickering and criticism was past. One reason why they were appealing for men was that in the ranks of the enemy there was not a single doubt but that victory would rest with them. Germany held that belief to-day—and it was sustained by generations of teaching—that her people were practically born to be the dominant voice in the world, the greatest world-power. To crush that out of Germany meant that the last ounce of power which our Empire possessed,- every blow which could be struck—on the bench in the factory, or on the field of battle—was needed, and that the great purse of our nation—un- equalled by. any other Empire—had to be opened wide. (Applause.) .

===== 0002601_19150911_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
"IN A NOBLE CAUSE."

The Mayor' (Alderman Peter Peacock), in proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. Crooks and the other speakers, said he had noticed with pride the way in which those in Warrington and district who had been bereaved by the war had borne up. They realised, it would k seem? that though they had lost those who were dear to them, they had died in a noble cause. "Do your duty," the Mayor concluded, "to your- selves and to the people of Warrington. War- rington had always done well, but we must eclipse all previous efforts, or else I shall be ashamed I was ever Mayor of a town like this." (Applause.) The meeting concluded with the singing by the audience of a verse of "Abide With Me," followed by the National Anthem.

NO COMPULSION

"Let Us Remember Triumphs Of Voluntary System."

On Tuesday the first procession left the Town Hall at noon, the marshal being Mr. J. W. -Hanson. Headed by the Band of the 2nd South Lancashires, they proceeded via Sankey-street, Bridge-street, Mersey-Street, Hall-street, Elles- mere-street, Howley-lane, Church-street, and Buttermarket-street, to Academy-street—where a meeting was held in the presence of a large crowd. The speakers' motor was lent by Mr. J. G. Withinshaw. Mr. J. W. Taylor (of Leeds) presiddd. The Chairman said he was not going to say which was the harder: duty to do—for the lads to enlist and go out, or for the parents to let them. But 'there weren't any of them in any part of life, when they came face to face with a duty and did it, who did not have a feeling come into their heart:' "Well, thank goodness, that job's done!" And there weren't any of them, if they came face to face with a duty and shirked it, didn't have a feeling of dissatisfaction. His wife—like all mothers who had let their lads go —was as "proud as a dog with two tails" because she had done her duty. If we didn't win, the Germans won, and then woe betide! He firmly believed that if the Germans got to this country our lives would not be, worth living. Fancy Warrington in the hands of the Germans! They, .would "spit in our faces, bit us under the lug. and call us dogs of Englishmen." Let them do their duty here and now. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150911_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
"IS IT NOT TIME?"

After a number of patriotic selections by the band, and the arrival amidst much cheering of the decorated tramcar laden with wounded.

Mr. S. H. Bibbings (of Sheffield) said Mr. Tay- lor and he came directly representing, through the Parliamentary Recruiting Committee, Lord Kitchener—the strong man of the Empire. They came to ask all physically fit and not engaged on munitions to enlist at once. Now that this war was 13 months old, was it not time that they laid it very seriously upon their con- science why they were not joining in the nation's help? (A Voice: " Cowards !") No! they must not try hooligan methods and call them cowards; he always believed the sitar method was better than the vinegar method. But if young men physically fit—knowing they had accepted and enjoyed the privileges of British citizenship for years—declined to accePt also the responsibilities, then let them give up the privileges.. (Hear, hear.) Men who had never seen Warrington had come to fight from all parts of the Empire, caring not for the, future— because they knew that, far away all over the world, where the British Empire was there was the utmost freedom for men, women and children.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
Two Warrington Lads.

- - - He was not unmindful of the fact tliat here in Warrington they had done marvelously well, but he knew also that here in Warrington there were plenty more who could go. Here was one of their own Warrington lads—Private Arthur Smith—who had been out and come back wounded; let them send more and more. Here was another—Private Mayze—a little drummer in_the South Lancashires, who was only fifteen years old. His size might not be great, he might not have many years, but he was wearing the most honourable uniform in the world—and if the pinch came, though his size might not be great , enough, they could guarantee that his heart would! He shook hands with both, and called them all to the holy crusade. Remember, to-day we were not fighting for Belgium! We went " into it"- for Belgium—

===== 0002601_19150911_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
LIVE VOLUNTARYISM!

Mr. W. Peter Rylands Says It

Cannot Be Beaten.

Mr. W. Peter Rylands presided at a largely- attended meeting held 'near the Lord Rodney Hotel, Winwick-street, on Tuesday evening. He was supported by Councillor W. H. J. Jenkins, Mr. F. L. Joseph (of Liverpool), Mr. E. A. Sanders, Mr. Herbert Hayes alma Mr. R. Gorton. Patriotic airs were played If Lord Derby's Recruiting Band, and we understand that close on a dozen recruits were obtained before the commencement of the meeting.

MR. RYLANDS AND THE END OF WAR. The Chairman observed at the outset that twelve months ago scarcely anyone believed that this war would outlast the present autumn. Yet it was, unfortunately, a fact which was staring us in the fact that the struggle showed no sign of coming to an end. It was certain that we still had a campaign of many weary and dreary months in front of us. This war differed from almost every war in history, owing to the fact that it was a war of peoples rather than of armies. In the old days, when a country went to war, a com- paratively small portion of the population formed the armed forces in the field, and the weapons used were not of a character to inflict the terrible losses which had now to be faced. All that had been changed, and the present war would, it was believed, be the last great war the world would experience, because it was un- thinkable that any nation would wantonly plunge the world into war again. , (Hear, allear.) Germany, France, Italy, Turkey and Russia had adopted a system of compulsion, by which they called upon every able-bodied man to represent them in the field. We, owing to our different conditions and institutions, relied upon the voluntary system, and everyone who knew what had been accomplished by it realised that the heart of England was still "beating true. (Applause.) A most extraordinary illus- tration had been given to the world of the unanimity and determination of the people, and, indeed, of our imperial race, as we had been enabled by the voluntary system to put into the field an army little short of that which had been raised under conscription. (Applause.) But we had not done yet. We were fighting for our national existence. If we lost this war our country would occupy a very small place in the councils of the world. Therefore the greater force we were able to put into the field the more raplilly would success attend our efforts. After twelve months' war 'we were beginning to understand what it meant, the calls it was making upon us and would make, and the time had, therefore, arrived when every man who was capable of carrying arms ought to be able to put his hand to his heart and say that he was doing something to help his country to victory. (Applause.) If one was making shells, uni- forms, providing food or getting caal out of the earth, or making barbed wire for entanglements, he was doing his duty, and no one Would criticise or blame him, as it was work which was as important as that which was being done in the field. But there were a large number of people who were not so engaged, and now that we realised the serious proposition against which we were up, everyone not engaged in the work he had mentioned should, if he was able, go to the Front. (Applause.) Day For Serious Endeavour.

"I don't believe in compulsion," Mr. Rylands concluded, "if our voluntary system will give us all we need. It has done great things up to the present, and I honestly believe it will carry us through, but what I should like would be a system of compulsory service in the sense that every man should be called upon to do somethir}g—either to make shells, guns and clothes tihat the troops want, or volunteer to go to the Front. Nothing can save us except de- termination and constancy of mind and endeavour. (Applause.) Our country has risen to the occasion in a manner which has given the greatest encouragement and hope to the whole of the Empire and, indeed, to the whole of the civilised human race, and it only remains for us to continue steadfast. Then, I believe, the day is not far distant when Germany will be com- pelled to admit defeat, and then will be the time to throw up our hats and rejoice. To-day is a time for serious endeavour, serious talk and constant determination to win." (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150911_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR ONE DETERMINATION.

Mr. Joseph observed that there never was a time in the history of our race when we were so united as we were at the present moment. The heart of the nation pulsated with .one de- termination, and that was again to express our inflexible and unalterable determination to spare no sacrifice, and to leave nothing undone which would crush Prussian militarism and make triumphant our cause—the ause of liberty and right.. (Applause.) This was a war against war, and we were out to lift from -Europe the shadow of a war which had been practically Jain upon it for 20 or 30 years. "In memory,' Mr. Joeph said in closing, "of those who have up to now given their lives for their country; in memory of the cause for which they have laid them down, be determined to help in every way you...can. in this struggle, so that we may come out of it purified and suc- cessful because those who will come after us will say that liberty and civilisation were in danger of being trodden under foot. Let the women -help all they can. Let them keep brave faces and cheery.- hearts, and influence the lads, •be- cause the breath of freedom is the only breath an Englishman can breathe." (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150911_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
SOLDIER'S APPEAL.

Gunner James Stanley Bradbury, of 28, West- street, who has spent eleven • months at the Front, and has participated in many big engagements,, appealed to all eligible men pre- sent to at once join the Colours. If they did so they would not only relieve those now in the trenches, but assist in bringing the war to a speedier conclusion. (Applause.) "God Save the King" and cheers for Gunner Bradbury, and "for the Warrington boys at the Front," concluded the proceedings.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
WHITECROSS RALLY.

Alderman Tinnion And The Pride Of The Future.

Alderman Tinnion presided over a meeting held at the corner of Green-street and Lovely- lane on Wednesday evening. The band of the 2nd South Lancs. Regiment—which was in at- tendance under the conductorship of Mr. R. E. Cahill—played ,several airs during the evening. In the course of his opening remarks,

, Alderman Tinnion said he was pleased to see so many yoting people present, for the result of this terrible crisis meant more to them than it did to the older people. Probably the latter had done their work, and their lives were almost over, but the lives of the children were only just starting. On them depended the maintenance of the traditions of the British Empire—an. Empire of which they had always been proud, but of which they were still prouder to-day. (Hear, hear.) A United Empire.

There never was a time when England, Scot- land, Ireland, Wales, Australia, New Zealand and all our other Colonies were so united and determined to carry on the struggle until we

had achieved a great and noble vistory. (Hear, hear.) Victory could not be gained, however, by going to sleep and trusting to muddle through. Germany had been preparing and organising for over forty years, in order to fight and make herself the predominant power in the world. It was time that we realised where we' were. They did not appeal to the men who were making munitions of war, for they were doing their duty just as well as the men in the trenches, and without them our soldiers would be useless. (Hear, hear.) Peace To Be Signed In Germany.

This recruiting campaign was opened primarily to recruit for the South Lancs. Regiment—a regiment of which they were all so proud. (Cheers.) Ti was up to the young men to go forward and join that regiment, and help to throw back out of Belgium those terrible German forces, and to invade Germany. "I myself," said Alderman Tinnion, "shall not be satisfied until the terms of peace are dictated to Germany in her own land. (Hear, hear.) I appeal to you to come forward and do your share. Think how proud your fathers, mothers and sisters will be in days to come when they will be able to say: 'Yes, our lad helped to rid the world of the Kaiser and his hordes.'" (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19150911_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
JEALOUS OF OUR COLONIES.

Mr. W. Toynbee said that after thirteen months of war we found Germany in possession of three-quarters of Belgium, about a quarter of Northern France, and the whole of Galicia and Poland, and in order to win this land they had perpetrated some of the most -awful horrors, which barbarians would not do. They had pre- pared for this war, and their object was not simply to dominate France, Belgium or Russia, but to make Great Britain a German colony— (hear, hear)—and to take all our Colonies, who were at the present time supplying the Mother Country with soldiers in a way that made our hearts beat with pride. (Hear, hear.) Ger- many had lost twenty-five per cent, of her in- dustrial population because she had not room for them, and as a result they said "We must have colonies, and we will have them, not in 't torrid part, but such places as 'Great Britain has her colonies to-day." The Germans were an industrial people, and they set out to make themselves the world dominant power. But before that &aid be accomplished there was one great-power which stood in their way—the great British Empire. (Cheers.) No Muddling Through This Time.

We had, however, always been prepared to muddle through, but they should all under- stand that we were faced to-day with the biggest proposition that we had ever faced be- fore. We are a most democratic country, and we are the most free and most liberty-loving country on the face of the earth. Our gallant soldiers and sailors had, for the last thirteen months, been fighting for our liberty, and had also been keeping our little island 'free from the invaders, and so to them we owed a great debt.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
Smash, Or Be Smashed.

Germany was out to crush us, and the only .1 way in which we could stop them from achiev- ing that end was by us first completely smash- ing them. (Hear, hear.) That, however, could not be done unless we supplied Lord Kitchener with the three necessaries which we promised we wduld do, viz., men, money and munitions. We were pledged to supply those three neces- saries. Munitions were being turned out at something like seven times the rate at which they were being turned out three months ago. Regarding the money part of the business, the Government would look after that. Only one thing was now left to us and that was to supply the men, and so help to clear from from the face of the earth that tyrant of Potsdam—the Kaiser. (Loud cheers.)

===== 0002601_19150911_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PRIVATE'S LAMENT.

Private Lees, .of the King's Own Yorkshire Light Infantry, who has been wounded, said a few words. In opening, he thanked the doctors and nurses of the Whitecross Military Hospital in giving •him and his colleagues such kind at- tention. As he walked through the streets he felt ashamed to- see such a number of young men walking about, while the men at the Front needed assistance So badly. "If they were to see the men, lying for fifty-four days in the trenches without a wash, shave or change of clothing, the young men would soon alter their minds and don the khaki or the blue—the finest clothes a young man could wear." (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19150911_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
FEWER CASUALTIES.

Councillor Osborne Tells How To Sa_ve Men's Lives.

On Wednesday evening the second procession —marshalled by Mr. R. Sumner, and headed by Lord Derby's Recruiting Band—left the Town Hall early, taking fo'r its route: Sankey, Butter- market, Dial, Fennel and Brick-streets, Battersby-lane, Orford-lane, and via Loigford- street to the Horse and Jockey Hotel. Opposite here a very large crowd congregated, attracted by the lively selections given by the band.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
TOWN COUNCIL RECORD.

Councillor Osborne (the chairman) said that meeting was being held for the specific purpose of getting men—men to replace the casualties and also to help in the trenches, so as to bring the war to a successful conclusion. Incidentally they would save loss /of life—because, para- doxical as it seemed, the more the rden, the less_ the casualties would be.

The previous night -1-1-; attended a meeting of the Town Council, where what he considered a record was created—they got through all the business in four minutes! Why? Because they were all of one mind! Now we were on with a tremendous undertaking, but if we were all of one mind we could get through the war in quick time. Three things needed to be accomplished, and to the superstitious it would be rather galling to know that they all began mith the 13th ,letter of the alphabet— viz., the letter M. Men, and still more men; Munitions, and still more munitions; Money, and more money! In all three War- rington had done well; but now they were out, for still more men. Women's Sacrifice.

Councillor Osborne next emphasised the work of the women in this great struggle. When one considered their heroism and sacrifice, and the splendid way in which they received the casualties from time to time, one felt how greatly they had helped in this business. It was "up to" every woman to see that her husband or brothers or sweetheart did their little bit, so that when asked they could say: "He's there !" (Applause.) THREE, NOT SEVENTEEN.

Lance-Corporal Knowles, of the 4th South Lancs. (and _a member of the Education Office staff home wounded), said he was as proud now as ever he had been in his life, and he advised the women if they had sons or brothers to induce them to go to the Front, so that in- stead of the soldiers out there having to stay seventeen days in the trenches they would only have to do three. How could they expect a man, when he had been seventeen days in the trenches to resist a bayonet charge? As he came to the meeting that night he saw a number of fellows cutting up bacon. Well, women could do that in their place if they would enlist. He was a married man, and his wife had been very anxious about him. He had even been reported dead; but he was still there, and as happy as he had ever been. But when "The TJnbreak- able 4th" came back they would not argue with those who had stayed at home; they would simply tell, them what they were—cowards. (Applause.) WHY SO MANY ARE WANTED. Lance-Corporal Wilson, of the Liverpool Scottish, said that if the young men wouldn't fight for their mothers, wives and sweethearts now he didn4 -know when they‘ would! Now they had for thirteenononths been begged and preached and prayed to. The Germans did not hate the Russians or the French, or the Belgians; they hated the English; and if they ever got over here there would be more suicides in one day than casualties in all the thirteen months. They might wonder why so many men were wanted when we already had three million. Well for instance, in the whole three years of the Boer War the Northumberland Fusiliers lost 500 men and 30 officers; in the present war, on June 16th, they lost more than those within half-an-hour. That was why we wanted more men.

All our Allies had conscription; but we had not; and he asked them: Would they like our Allies to say at the end of the war that England had

not done her full " whack "? Because until we had every possible man doing his utmost, we should not be doing our full "whack." War- ringtonians had no right to say "Bravo, South Lancs." until they knew that every possible man was in it. When the war first started we had no difficulty about recruiting; the idea was: "Boys, there's a row on somewhere; we're in it!" But that good old English spirit was being lost; and it was a disgrace that he, as a wounded soldier and one of their own men, should have to ask them to come outi. It was not because they lacked pluck, and they had the finest land and traditions to fight for; then let them keep the glorious old flag flying. (Applause.) The Chairman announced that they had already got six recruits from the meeting, now motoring to town to enlist. Via Winwick-road, Winwick-street, Horse- market-street and Sankey-street, the procession —followed by some hundreds— _ten returned to the Town Hall, where a short speech was de- livered and musical items given: The speakers' motor was lent by Mr. W. Peter Rylands.

Mr. Fulton-Smith Discourages

OUR TERMS OF PEACE.

The Chairman (Mr. J. Fulton-Smith) said that England was determined to win this war. A number of people found fault because they did not understand, and the men did not come

round as they would like. They blamed the women, but the women of this country had been making sure quietly and successfully that our men already at the Front were well equipped; and they had spared no money, time or industry to secure the comfort of the soldiers day after clay. They had determined that nothing should be left. undone to secure the soldiers rood, clothing and everything else - required. The duty of every man, woman and child was. to do a little; everybody doing his duty and not grumbling and making other people miserable would encourage others to look with hope to the time when England would make peace on her own terms—a peace whies would last hundreds of years. She would maV peace on no other terms! Everyone, if not fighting or on work for the - Government or munitions might become Volunteer like himself—for the tune would come when every man would be called upon to defend this country and everything that it stood for. They a4ked them to help each other, and when they saw any young man hesitating about joining the Army, they should encourage him and not scoff—and the better it would be for the success of Lord Kitchener's Anal- (Applause.) WHY RECRUITS ARE RARE. Mr. F. L. Joseph (of Liverpool), said one out- standing fact of their campaign in Warrington had been the large crowds and great enthusi- asm; and yet they had had very few eligible men to appeal to. Their number of recruits , was small, and the reason was' that Warrington was doing so magnificently in munitions. It was the duty of every man on war-work to stick- to his 'job—half of the country's industry was now turning out munitions and equipment for our Army, and that showed the nature and extent of the war. This was a "family job," and all were now prepared to make sacrifices for the common good. What had brought about this altered spirit? We had come at last to understand that unless we won through iv° should be a subject race and tyranny would be-- come enthroned. The proper place for a ' croaker was in the mud; let them have no time or intercourse with any man who didn't think I we should win; it was better to die a hero than live a coward.

THE TWO ALTERNATIVES.

Mr. Bibbings (of Sheffield), in a fine anq eloquent speech, said there were those who sain no one could tell how the war could be ended. But they cOuld end it by Saturday night—it was Thursday now, early closing—simply by com- municating with the Germans: "Gentlemen, we have sinned. We have fought you and we are sorry. Please forgive us and be as kind as youl can." (Laughter.) But the other way of end" ing the war was for every man to remember what had happened to bring us into it—the work of this set of savages who had blasphemed the very name of civilisation! Let us fire our imaginations with what we were up against- The Germans only real aim was to devastate England. We were not only a country but ark Empire; but why had these black-faced lad! come 13,000 miles over the sea to fight for us r Because wherever the British flag Hew tlieY. knew there was true and utmost freedom! If they would do that what should the British lads do? (Loud applause.) THE REV. S. F. COLLIER.

It is natural that the war should affect the 'various Churches, causing very often unexpected inconveniences. The Bible Classes in all our schools have suffered, as might have been ex- pected. The Sunday schools in some places have suffered, chiefly through the absence of teachers, and the demand that has to be made on all for overtime work. In a similar way .choirs are suffering. May we point out that it would be a great boon in many parishes if those who are in a position to do emergency work would come forward to offer temporary help to relieve the situation.—Holy Trinity Parish Magazine.

Completion Of Thirty Years' Ministry.

The Rev. S. F. Collier preached on Sunday 'evening before ,a large congregation in the Albert Hall, Manchester, .on the occasion of en- tering upon the 'thirty-first year of his superin- 'tendency of the Manchester and Salford Wes- leyan Mission. The Mission has greatly tileveloped during the last thirty years, until it has become one of the most extensive religious -and social agencies in the country. The head- quarters of the work are at the Central Hall, Oldham-street, which was built after Mr. Col- lier entered upon his great work, and within the last few years the Albert Hall and Aston Institute have been established in Peter-street, and have added means to the effective carrying on of the Mission's many activities.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
NATIONAL EXPERIENCE.

Old Testament Interpreted At A Wycliffe Anniversary.

But here comes the point: Joseph's brethren never believed that he had forgiven them! they thought he was biding his chance. Little minds don't understand great minds; little natures find it impossible to rise to the standard of great souls.

The words formed part of one of the powerful siermons preached at Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday. The preacher was the Rev. E. Griffith Jones—principal of the Yorkshire United College, Bradford, and the celebrated author of "The Ascent Through Christ "—and the occasion the 42nd anniversary of the church dedication. Good congregations assembled. Genesis 1. 19 and 20: "And Joseph said unto kis brethren, Fear not: for am I in the place 'Of God? But as for you, ye thought evil against me; but God meant it unto good, to 'bring to pass, as it is this day, to save much people alive." Having read this morning text, Mr. Griffith Jones said he had no doubt it had happened with them, as with many others dur- ing these solemn and tragic days, that many passages of the Bible—especially of the Old 'Testament—had suddenly leapt into prominence and taken upon themselves a new meaning. As one read and sang these memorial narratives and passages, one realised from other literary expression of national experience that they had been put to their last resources, that the Psalms were really battle songs ringing out the crash of victory and the horror of ltress—of One Who alone could remain as a re .f.tilge and succour for the stricken soul, a az.°llg tower. But during the past few months "," of the passages of Scripture which had irllek him had been the story of Joseph; not 4.1110/10 because of its interest or its historic nth, but because of its deeper meaning. It was inspired because it was equally true of Ilational experience and of life as a whole.

Evil Divinely Meant.

After recounting the story of Joseph, the preacher .said one could imagine this man from the height of his greatness looking back upon hie chequered career, and seeing the hand of God in all:---even the cruelty of his brethren toward him was necessary to his life and to his becoming Prime Minister of Egypt. So he argued that even those things were divinely meant to happen; and the time when he felt that he had no friend in heaven or earth was leading to the time when he should have hosts of friends, the brethren who had acteld, so evilly towards him dying on his heart, folded to his bosom as his dearest friends. Yet his brethren never believed that he had really for- given. them! "The light shone through the darkness, and the darkness comprehended it 1)ot." Joseph knew it, he was a shrewd man of the world, as well as a pious saint. "When we Itre puzzled about our difficulties in life," went on the preacher, "let us learn from this story that we cannot come to any great. event or pro- vidential point in the history of the world with- out afterwards, in the light of events, seeing the Purpose of God. When we have strong enough f aith to carry us through successfully, we shall Ivin to God.'l

Four Points.

here they had a practical example of the 'Christian belief in providence, from which four Points might be taken. (1) "Ye thought evil against me, but God meant it unto good." Here Was a moral of the two great opposing forces of life--evil and good. (2) These two great ex- Periences, incompatible though they were; were aoniehow interwoven. . (3) In this world one could not change evil into good, nor good into but somehow one may be ancilliary or contributory to the other. (4) God could not do this without their co-operation. And they had '!0 put themselves in the stream of God before they could bring themselves to the work of the Illation. Now a short time ago they heard a lot about evil being a cloak of holiness, but he did not think they would hear anything more about that in the future. With reference to the first „Point: Evil was a a bad will positively acting zrom no side,, and good was a holy will acting Positively from the other. (2) They moved be- tween the two eternal forces—the warp and Woof in the loom of life, on which their will was the shuttle. (3) This was the important -Point: One could be made to serve the other. Profoundest Mystery of Universe.

• Now they were up against the profoundest laYstery of the universe—What was God's rela- :Lion to the evil in the world? 'Was it here 'against His will? Then He would be powerless llad not the Omnipotent! Was it here by His will? Then He could do away with it, and they ' W. ould have no more evil in the world! Or was ,14 here in some strange and mysterious *ay by ,°,d's ordinance because He had some good aims Znich could not be brought about in any other ;aY? The human mind was jiot able to give i;) eompletely satisfactory 'solution of this pro- because it had no parallel; there were 'vrifficulties whichever wny they turned. But tiller' they consulted history .and found how I,,,,thgs came about, they could see that, indeed, od meant it unto good." .tien the sacrifice our Lord was made possible by the evil which ii/..erYwbere surged around the Cross, and the 6,aholical Malice which led to His betrayal. of that background the Cross shone, the

very symbol of forgiving love. And that was not doubtful theology! It was historic fact.

" This Is Our Comfort."

This was their comfort in these terrible and trying times. Just now they were seeing what they were compelled to believe were evil forces— horrors unspeakable and indescribable. The heavens were black with men's iniquities; and history told of nothing more diabolical than the plot which had brought about this war. But evil was not the only actor in the drama! His purpose was being slowly accomplished through it all. One could only see the beginning of the grief yet; but presently they would see more and more clearly God's footsteps in the storm. Loyalty and devotion they would see in un- expected directions; and the old passion for frivolity and pleasure had had a severe check, so that after the war could hardly return as before. This war had brought forth the basest passions, but it had also called forth the finest sacrifice and nobility. They would yet be able to say that "God meant it unto good "; a greater future was still before the world!

===== 0002601_19150911_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
MISTAKES OF PAST CENTURY.

In the evening Mr. Griffith Jones discoursed on some of the mistakes and failures of the past century, and pointed out the duty which awaited the people when the present European struggle was over. What was wanted in the world to- day, he said, was a true application of the spirit of the Gospel—a new faith in rich and Apoor. European civilisation in the 19th century dis- covered new sources of power for mankind, but it failed to moralise the use of that power; and it would be the task of the coming era to remedy that terrible omission. The era just closed would always be remembered as the era in which man conquered the forces of nature for "his own personal benefit, and turned them to his own benefit.

Yet in spite of the wonderful inventions of machinery, man had not been made really happy. Such inventions only served to quicken the appetite for sensation, excitement and novelty. They were intended to lighten human labour, but they had made it more monotonous. and had added to the stress and competition of life. A feeling of unrest had fallen upon the civilised world. It was a disappointed and dis- enchanted world. This wonderful power had been exploited by the few and not in the inter- ests of the many.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
Nature, Not Man.

The essential task in front of them was to remedy this state of things, and make man's lordship over nature impossible in the future. The governments of the world had been the chief sinners, but the people must see to it that the representative authorities should not steer these forces of nature in the direction of their own class, and they should devise means of making this abuse impossible.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
Secret of Accumulation.

European civilisation in the 19th century dis- covefed the secret of accumulating wealth, and it would be the task of the next period in human history to master the secret of a more equal distribution. Such accumulation was the direct result of the substitution of machinery. Until quite modern times the only wealth producing machine was the human body, which was in- capable of doing more than a definite amount of work. The introduction of scientific machinery, however, multiplied the rate which wealth courd be produced, and it was impossible to place any limit on the rate wealth might be produced by machinery. That process had been going on in Europe and in every civilised land, unchecked and uncontrolled—resulting in friction, discon- tent and revolt. The time was coming when this accumulation of wealth shall be more fairly distributed amongst the community.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
ACTIVE CHURCHMEN.

Men's Society Show Keenness At Federation Meeting.

Some have recently raised the question whether the Church of England's Men's Society is really and spiritually alive. An observer present at the meetings of delegates, &c., com- posing the Warrington and District Federation would certainly not complain of any lack of keenness or interest. These meetings occur once every two months at different centres, and are eagerly looked.- forward to and appreciated by those who attend.

-One of such meetings took place ,at Widnes last Saturday, in t. building attached to St. Mary's Church. Some forty or fifty represen- tatives from neighbouring parishes met tq dis- cuss matters of mutual and general interest, and, under that -able Chairman, the Rev. C. E. Greenham (vicar of Halton), settled down to the first part of their programme, to deal with business connected with the Federation.

Having confirmed minutes drawn at the last similar, meeting, held at Lymm, and at the annual general meeting, at Halton in June (an account of which appeared in "The Exam- iner "), one crisp and business-like resolution followed another in quick succession: The chief among these propositions were aimed at effecting improvements in the system of select- ing Federation delegates to the annual London Conference; fixing definite dates for the six meetings yearly of the Federation (it was re- solved to convene these on the first Saturday in February, April, June, August, October' and December); and re-establishing the financial position of the Federation, which was tempor- arily embarrassed by the war (it was resolved to make a ss. levy on each branch): It was also agreed that at future Federation meetings the customary address to delegates should be omitted. The wisdom of this course will be apparent to all, as .it allows of more time for discussing vital matters affecting the life of the branches.. Few arguments for the retention of the address could be more powerful than the excellence and helpfulness of that delivered on Saturday (for the last time).

===== 0002601_19150911_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
ARE WE DECADENT?

For vigour and thoroughness of treatment it has rarely been our lot to listen to a speaker able to bring such qualities to bear on the time- honoured subject) "Are we, as a nation, decadent." The Lecturer (Mr. A. R. Smith, of Farnworth) took a strong affirmative attitude and vividly pictured the present-day all-absorb- ing lust for pleasure, in spite of the war's insistent demands. He also dealt with the degenerating physical condition, of the rape, and incapability of bearing hardilip; the decay of chivalry; the decline of home influence; rest- lessness and flippancy in literary pursuits, as instanced by the desire for publications dealing with odds and ends, instead of standard' works by authors of merit. The address concluded with an exposure of irreligion prevalent to-day, and appealed for greater virility and originality above all in religious matters on the part of the •clergy and spiritually-minded laymen, which members of the C.E.M.S. professed.them- selves to be.

Light refreshments were afterwards served by the St. Mary's (Widnes) Branch, the hosts for the evening, to whom votes of thanks were ten- dered for their hospitality, as were also accorded to Mr. A. R. Smith for his telling address, and the Rev. C. E. Greenham for his conduct of, the 'fleeting. On Saturday, October 2nd, the Federation will be the guests of St. Paul's Parish, War- rington Branch, and on December 4th will meet at St. Thomas', Stockton Heath, details of which will be announced later.

AUGUST IMPORTS AND EXPORTS

Last month's imports into the United King- dom are returned by the Board of Trade as having been of the value of £69,496,695, an increase of 1'27,153,988 on the figures for the first month of the war, and of £13,520,991 as compared with those for August, 1913. The in- crease was principally in food and drink, wood and timber, and wool. The exports were .t 32,438,855 or .t 8,227,584 more than last year's, but 0g11,67,1,874 less than those of August, 1913. Cotton abd woollen goods, coal, and iron and steel showed considerable increases.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

War, Women, and Work. Germany is now sending her grandfathers to the war, and there is no doubt that her 'Superior`organisation for the use of women in industry is one of her sources of strength. The first essential is to separate the work that is suitable for women from that which is too heavy for them to undertake; but, although we are making little progress in that direction, there is going on a large and steady replace- ment of men by women, and by universal cansent the substitution has made little or no difference in the efficiency of. the work. Women seem to be more at home on the railways than in agricultural employments, and whilst the labourers are few, harvesting is heavy work for women, at least for all but the strongest. Hop-picking is an exception, and despite re- ports of a light crop, this month will see the exodus of thousands of women and children from the poorest districts in London to the Kentish hop, gardens. Unpatriotic Skirts.

Dame Fashion is in. a chastened mood. The war has broken her spirit, and she seems inclined to put up with any rebuff: When war commenced the Kaiser ordered her to remove from Paris to Berlin, but she took no notice, and German women are being reproached for slavishly adopting the under-skirt which hails from the enemy. It is very unpatriotic, be- cause the narrow skirt takes so , much less material, and therefore makes less demand on the restricted resources of the Fatherland. To this appeal the German women have made no response, but it would not- be astonishing if Western modes came to their rescue, and put them in tight skirts vain. Notwithstanding all the efforts of the fashion-mongers to place it out of date, there is a widespread reluctance to give up the Convenient, light, more healthy and economical narrow skirt, and the measure of success attained by this or that mode, is not

to be measured by what happens at the top of the social ladder, but how far it will be folz, lowed by the "common people."

War and the Drapery Trade: Whilst war economy is seriously affecting tfie Court dressmakers, the less exclusive estab- lishments and shops Catering for the middle- classes are doing very well. They are benefiting both ways. Women who formerly reckoned to pay five or six guineas for a dress are buying three-guinea ready-to-wear costumes, whilst there is an almost unprecedented demand for the cheaper ready-mades, owing to war money being plentiful among the working classes. It is the same in every department of the drapery trade; they are, generally speaking, doing a flourishing business. Nor is there any notice- able falling off in their bright and attractive appearance. When war broke out we were told that mew's and women's attire would soon' become of a monotonous sameness of drabs ' and browns, owing to the cutting off of Ger- man dyes. Judging from the shop windows, we are still far from being reduced to such an extremity, although there is no doubt that the shortage of dyes wiTT have a marked effect on the clothing trade.. Autumn Modes.

The velvet hem, in some cases very broad, will finish autumn and winter skirts of soft woollen materials. A new make of cashmere is shown that will be extensively used in this way. It is rather heavy, and has a particu- larly smooth, silken surface this is very attrac- tive. Velvet sleeves to match the hems give to these dresses a seaspnable degree of warmth lacking in the transparencieS that have been worn during the .last four cr five winters. Moderation is the •keynote of autumn skirts, which will be neither unreasonlbiy w ide nor ridiculously short. Coats accompanying skirts will be longer than last winter, and in order to harmonise with the fullness below them will have full bascpiesT .Fortunately these longer coats, as a rule, are much more becom- ing to the average woman than the short sac shape. Notwithstanding the difficulties experi- enced in obtaining a good indigo dye, fine navy blue serge coatings will continue to be among

the most popular vinter fabrics for hard wear suits,

Rest Gowns c 4 Ninri gnd Brocade.

The wise woman who took advantage of the 'sales,- to buy a piste of beautiful brocade, ordinarily prohibitive in price, is now turning it to good account. With the aid of a short length of ninon, a really beautiful and most useful rest gown of the more elaborate type is produced. Usually the brocade is 4wathe,d round the figure, then arranged to fall in a point behind, where it is fastened by a large, old-fashioned piece of jewellery, or a clasp. Brocade of a very vivid colouring may be veiled if preferred. Rest gowfll3. show some new features this autumn. Far instance, when intended for dinner or evening wear, a train ' is sometimes added. But ft is narrow and detachable. The corsage fits the figure more closely, and the dress, which has no unneces- sary fastenings, slips on more easily than hitherto. As autumn advances; wool-back satin and other more substantial fabrics will be used for these comfortable and indispen- sable garments. Fashionable Coiffures.

The drastic change in the silhouette has, ITO t affected the style of dressing the hair to any great extent. In fact there has been less change—a few freakish fashions excepted—this- year than last. The tendency becomes more marked every day to adopt an individual style, consequently there is much more variety in coiffures than hitherto. The girl with a very high forehecid will rejoice dila fringes have returned, but the heavy, straight kind is de rigueur, even when the rest of the hair is waved. Women fortunate enough to possess prettily-shaped heads brush their locks closely and smoothly instead of hiding the outline under puffs and chignons. Waved hair ia still very much worn, and a great improvement has taken place recently in the methods of producing it. The " bobbed " style suits a piquant face, although to cut off luxuriant tresses in order to follow a fleeting fashion is certainly not a- wise proceeding. The classic style has a following, but should be adopted only by women dowered with a perfect. profile. To others its severity proves unbecomingly trying.

HOME NOTES.

Profitable Recipes and Useful ' Suggestions.

The Price of Tea.

Tea is not expected to become cheaper, in spite of heavy shipments of nearly 20,000,0001 b. from Ceylon and of 6,000,0001 b. from India. There has, says the "Times," been a slight fall in the less expensive teas, but this, accord- ing to several of the largest retail houses in London, is nothing; there was plenty of room for a drop, as market prices had advanced considerably, in addition to the rise caused by the increase in the tea duty in November last. It is stated that owing to various special cir- cumstances, such as the very greatly increased consumption in our own country and also in France and Russia, and the unprecedented requirements of the War Office for the supply of troops at home and abroad, enormous con- tracts for which are given out almost weekly, there has been an abnormal rise in market values. Common tea realises about is. per lb. in bond and is. Bd. per lb. duty paid. It is curious to note that while France is using more tea owing to its ease of preparation, in the United Kingdom the consumption of coffee has risen.

Keeping Butter Cool. It is most important that butter should be kept cool in hot weather. To do this, place it in a bowl, then put the bowl and butter into a larger dish containing as much salt and water as will come .to within half an inch of the tap of the butter-bowl. Cover lightly with a piece of white paper or muslin to keep out the dust, and put in a cool place. Always before unwrapping butter hold the parcel under the cold-water tap for a few seconds, and you will find that the paper will come off without even the slightest bit of butter adhering. Mushrooms, To Grill.

Choose large, mushrooms of equal size. Peel them, and cut off the ends of the stalks. Wash in salted water, and dry thoroughly in a cloth. Slightly score the rounded side of the mush- rooms and lay-them on a deep plate. Sprinkle them with pepper and salt and pour some melted butter or salad oil over them. Let them lie for half an hour, basting them occasion- ally with butter. Then lay them on a hot broiler, which has been brushed over with a little of the oil, and broil them over or in front of a clear fire, or under the grill of a gas stove. The mushrooms should be turned once during the cooking. Serve them on small pieces of hot buttered toast and sprinkle a little lemon juice over each. Baked Mushrooms.

Peel large, fregh mushrooms and put into a buttered baking-dish. Sprinkle with salt and pepper and put a small lump of butter on each one. Bake for ten or fifteen minutes in a quick oven, basting frequently with melted butter. Add a few drops of lemon juice and serve hot on the same dish. A little mace and minced, parsley may be added to the seasoning. Stewed Mushrooms.

Peel and trim half a pound of fresh mush- rooms. Fry in butter, season with ,salt and pepper, dredge with flour, and add half a cupful of hot water or stock. Cook slowly for five minutes. Three-fourths cupful or more of cream may be used for liquid, and a little 'grated nutmeg may he added to the seaponing.

If hot water is used, season with minced parsley and onion-juice.

Fish Balls.

The remnants of any cold fish can be used for fish balls. Remove all the skin and bones and shread very fine. Add an equal quantity of mashed potatoes, make into a stiff batter with piece of butter and some milk, and a beaten egg; add salt, shape into &Ails and fry in lard, or drippings, to a light brown. . Lentil Pie.

Ingredients: 6oz. of red lentils, 2oz. of good dripping, Soz. of grated cheese—dry pieces will do for this-30z. of breadcrumbs, 3oz. of onion, one 031espoonful- of chopped parsley. Method: Soak the lentils for some hours in cold water, and then drain them. Chop the onions and fry them in hot fat; then add the lentils and half a pint of boiling water or weak stock, and cook slowly until the lentils are soft and tender adding more liquid if required. Now add the cheese, parsley, and enough bean gravy to moisten well. Turn all into a greased pie- dish, sprinkle thickly with crumbs, and bake in a moderate oven until nicely browned. To make the bean gravy needed, soak about four tablespoonfuls of haricot beans overnight, then drain them, and put them into a saucepan with 11 pints of water, a sliced carrot, a piece of onion, a good-sized tomato, and a little celery- salt, and pepper. Simmer until the beans are tender; then rub through a sieve, add season- ing to taste, and use. To make this a rich and appetising brown colour add a few drops of home-made caramel.
