NEXT WEEK'S ESSAY: "HOW WOULD

YOU SPEND TEN SHILLINGS?"

MY DEAR CHILDREN,-

I declare I can picture you all taking up your pens straight away when I tell you what an easy competition .we have for next week.. It is: "If you were given ten shillings how would you spend it?" My word! just fancy how fine it would be! Write only a short letter, please, and let me have it before Tuesday. I want you all ,to go in for ithis very easy com- petition. ABOUT THE WAR. --

Now in coming to our essays about the war, I must .say that one thing sarprisekl me very much indeed—you all looked on .the sad side! Well, the war is, of course, a very sad business indeed, and when we think of all the broken hearts it nearly makes our .hearts break too. But we have all to remember that we simply must look on the bright side! The worst thing we can do is to look on the sad side; and when I meet those whining people who always carry a tear in their eye I just feel aS, if I want. to knock them down. But, dear me! Why, after all, should we want tot be downhearted? Just think of all the things we have to be glad about! Just,think of all the horrible things .that might be happening to and yet .are not.•. , We might be losing, instead. of *inning all the *eV ! We 'might be starving, instead of having felt no shortage of

food! London might have been bombarded and burnt down (as the Kaiser wants), yet it is still there! We might have had a bad King, in- stead of a good one! We might have had bad Cabinet Ministers, but they are all good men (at least, NNT say so in war time) ! I will say no more about the war now. Only you should never forget that in everything, how- ever great or small, there is always more to be glad about than to be sad about. Would yOu believe that once I heard a man say that though his old mother had died he felt quite happy when. he thought of how • she might have died twenty years earlier! It was .0 curious thing to say, wasn't it? Yet it showed the right spirit; and I want' you all to be glad. Already we have one rule in our Corner: "Write your best"; hereby I make another: "Always be glad." This week's Otters, then, are so upsetting that I am only going to print the winning letter, and all the others I have thrown away without read- ing even twice! The winner is

HERE AND THERE.

Bridget Was Glad.

Mistress: "Bridget, that jug you broke this morning belonged to my great-grandmother!" Bridget: "I'm glad to hear that. I was afraid it was something ye had bought lately."

Deserved Some Credit. "It was Satan," said a mother to one of her children, "who put it into your head to pull Elsie's hair."

"Perhaps it was," replied the hopeful, "but kicking her shins wap my own idea." Hot Flatiron a Germ Killer.

To men the flatiron seems a thing of evil. Its use renders the house uninhabitable one day out of seven; it distracts woman's attelition from the all important matter of preparing food. In , conspiracy with starch it renders clothes uncomfortable, turns napkins into slippery boards, and -banishes sleep from beds. Civilised man is a slave of the ironing board, and the boiled shirt is the emblem of his degradation. Now the scientists say that ironing has an important function as an antiseptic; that the hot iron is one of our leading germ killers. The instrument of torture may have a temperature of 266 degrees, and that, they say, is more than enough to satisfy the most fastidious bacillus. In places where sterilizing devices are not handy surgical dressing* may be ironed with great advantage, and in a recent experiinent clothing which has been worn by diphtheria patients was completely disinfected by the use of a hot iron. Long suffering man will note with relief that the scientists have said nothing in defence of starch. There has been no vindication of the boiled shirt. ,

HINTS ON HEALTH.

Useful Information In Popular Language.

Excess of Flesh Foods.

A million and a half people die in the United States every year. Half of them die from chronic diseases. Probably it is not too much to say that one-third of all these people lose their lives prematurely because of errors in diet, which might be easily corrected. Of all dietetic errors, one of the most potent in the production of chronic disease is the large consumption of meat which has developed in the last half-century, during which the con- sumption of meat has increased to three or four times the amount formerly consumed.— Dr. Kellogg. The Sleep Needed. During the first two months of life a healthy, well-managed baby will sleep almost all the time, waking only at the regialar feeding times, when probably a cry will give notice of the fact. Once in the twenty-four hours it should be a lusty fit of crying, so that the lungs may be well expanded, and good exer- cise given generally. The time when sleep should\be least encouraged is in the late hours of the day, as by that means a sounder and longer sleep is induced at night. At six months old two-thirds of the day of twenty-four hours should be spent in sleep, which means that sixteen hours is the proper time. This should be continued until the first birthday, when a couple of hours less suffices, ten hours being dbvoted to the waking time. This fourteen hours' sleep is needed by children until they are five years old, after which about two hours may be knocked off (which means practically in many cases dropping the sleep by day and having only the long night of twelve hours), and after the seventh birthday still another hour may be stolen from night for day, and this eleven hours should be continued• on until the small person is ten or eleven years old.— "Queen." To Prevent Excessive Sweating. People who sweat excessively usually find the palms of the hands .and feet most often affected—sometimes to such an extent as to give to both the hands and the feet a parboiled appearance. To get relief from disagreeable odours take each night and morning an alter- nate hot and cold foot bath. This is taken by dipping the feet, first into the hot and then into the cold water, every half minute for fifteen or twenty minutes. Wipe the feet dry and apply a strong decoction of white-oak bark, or a solution of tannin in water, two , drachms to the ounce. Or, better still, is a ' solution of tannin and glycerine in the same proportion. The old boots or shoes saturated with perspiration should be disused, and a new pair purchased. The stockings should be changed every day. Rubbers and other imper- vious foot coverings should not be worn, or should be kept on as short a time as possible.— X-ray treatments; given by a competent specialist, also effect speedy cures of this dis- ease. A good remedy is thorough cleansing of the parts, at least twice a day, with soap and water, or a disinfectant lotion, such as per- manganate of potash, a solution of chlorinated soda, or with two or three per cent. of carbolic acid. Washing the affected parts with a solu- tion of chloral, a drachm to the ounce, is a recently recommended remedy. "Good Health."

RED CROSS LADIES.

Mrs. Eilocatta Presents Certifi- cates At Town Hall.

NEW DETACHMENT.

At the Town Hall on Saturday afternoon took place a private but exceptionally interesting func- tion—a presentation of First Aid Certificates in connection with the Daresbury Division British Red Cross, Society. The certificates were awarded on the results of an examination con- ducted some few weeks ago, in which 98 per cent. of Dr. Adams's class were successful; Without delay a new detachment is being formed for hospital service. _ .

- Amid a very good attendance Miss Sylvia Broadbent (vice-president of the division) occu- pied the chair, and expressed apology for absence on behalf of the Mayor and Mayoress, Dr. Bowden (examiner), Captain Burton (assist- ant Cheshire Director), Miss Izod, Nurse Phillips, Nurse Davy, Mrs. Bower, Mrs. Segar Owen and others.

SOLEMN TRUST AND PRIVILEGE.

Miss Sy'Via Broadbent, in welcoming Colonel and Mrs. Mecatta, said there was a very pleas- ant appropriateness in the fact that before the outbreak of war the Colonel was working for the B.R.C.S. as hon. county secretary for Cheshire. It was, however, with great regret that they missed the presence of Lady Harmood Banner-- one of whose sons bad given his life for his country. _ _ _

It was topteasure to know that Dr. Adams had been very pleased with his class, and that 98 per cent. passed the examination. (Hear, hear.) This was quite a severe test given by Dr. Bow- den, and no easy walk-over! This was as it should be: Red Cross work was a very serious matter, and they did not wish anyone to take it up lightly; but to realise that the care of the wounded was a solemn trust, and a great privi- lege, to be carried out with a deep sense of responsibility. Necessity of Discipline. They now had material, she hoped, for an- other Vefuntary Aid Detachment in connection with the Daresbury Division of Cheshire. She would take this opportunity of impressing upon candidates the importance, and, in fact, the absolute necessity of obedience\and loyalty to, and co-operation with, the officers of their de- tachment. The composition of a detachment was: Commandant, medical officer, lady super- intendent. quartermaster, four cooks and fifteen nurses. She intended to submit the following names to the Divisional Committee: Miss Nora Joynson as commandant; Mr. Falkiner, R.A.M.C., as medical officer; and Nurse Phillips as lady superintendent. The speaker went on to pay fine tributes to the work of these three.

In peace the regulatioiis! were that each de- tachment should meet for practice at least twerre times during the year, and that in war- time it should serve some hospital. In the pres- ent case monthly meetings might be held under the superintendence of the medical officer or lady superintendent, and when the detachment was called upon for service it should be mobil- ised for that purpose. " Warrington In Cheshire."

Miss Broadbent then explained why it was that Warrington, a Lancashire town., should be incorporated with a 'Cheshire Division. A few months ago there had been a feeling in the town that it would be well to have a War- rington Division, or that Warrington should form part of a Lancashire Division. She had tried to arrange this, but the headquarters pre- ferred to leave the organisation as it was—as the two districts were 'so much connected and their interests interwoven. This seemed to work quite naturally. The Daresbury Division—in addition .to its ?members, associates and adherents—i?ossessed before the war two fully-organised voluntary aid detachments, registered respectively in 1912 and 1913; their commandants being Miss M. Broadbent of Cheshire 22 and Miss Monks of ICheshire 48. These two detachments provided the staff of Raddon Court Red Cross Hospital, as well as supplying some nurses for White- cross, The Oaklands and Thelwall Heys Hos- pitals, three for war service outside the district, and nurses to help at the Orford Barracks Hospital every . morning. During the war a new detachment had been trained, registered and mobilised—namely Cheshire 128, command- ant Miss Ewart—and was working at the Whitecross Hospital. She had now great pleasure in informing this detachment that Dr. J. Joseph had consented to be their medical officer. (Applause.) She could not yet say where they were likely to be called upon to work, but there would be ample opportunity for service among the wounded soldiers who were sacrificing health and life for them. (Applause.)

MRS. MOCATTA'S HOPE.

Mrs. Mocatta, who was very heartily received,. spoke of the great pleasure it had afforded her to present the certificates and badges. By giv: mg up their time to the work of nursing the wounded, they were doing something for their country and showing that spirit of sacrifice which had been shown by the soldiers and sailors, as well as by their doctors and nurses. Slie,was sure they would do all in their power to make themselves proficient, and it must be very gratifying to their president to add another dettschment to the division. She added her heartiest wishes to them and everybody acting for the hospitals.' (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150911_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
A "STIFF" EXAMINATION.

Colonel Mocatta, who proposed thanks to the crass lecturer and the examiner, said they must aft feel they had been very good in giving up their valuable time, for in times like these every doctor in the country was hard at work.. Ninety-eight per cent had passed the examine- tibn Well, in ordinary times they might have felt that that meant the examination had not been very severe; but now they knew it was. Tie added thanks to Miss Sylvia Broadbent—to whom everybody owed a great debt of gratitude for the way in which she organised the branch in peace time. Had that not been done, it ' would not have been possible to commence work as quickly and as efficiently, as they did when the war broke out. He had paid several visits Ito Raddon Court Hospital and had seen how well everything was worked out; he had also beard tributes from the men themselves. (Ap- plause.)

===== 0002601_19150911_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT THE MEETING WISHED.

Mr. Falkiner, R.A.M.C. (assistant registrar at the Lord Derby War Hospital, Winwick) seconded, and, spoke of the war. They had, he believed, been too haughty and selfish., Any intelligent person who had been reading the papers for the last two years must have known what was coming; and to intelligent people it had been a grievous burden to have to bear, in addition to the ordinary burdens of life. could not help saying what they all felt: "That this meeting wishes for the extermination of Hick blood-guilty Kaiser and all his venomous gang." (Laughter and applause.)

HELP FROM ST. JOHN AMBULANCE. Dr. Adams, who was cordially receivelL__ acknowledged the vote; and mentioned that, he had been associated with the St. John Ambu- lance since "about 1885, but through all those years this was the first function he rememberel. to have been held; he thought it wAs a gooit thing, as it brought them all together. Of the class itself he must say a few words. When, he began, he had just conic back invalided from the Front; however, he decided to meet the "twenty or thirty" he had been told of, and: when he got there it wasn't far off a hundred! (Laughter.) The class had, indeed, grown to such a size that an appeal to withdraw wan made to those who did not intend to continue the work afterward. In turn he moved thanks, to Mrs. Mocatta. (Apalause.) Miss S. Broadbent tWitnked Miss Izod (absent through bereavement) for her secretarial work; Mr. Baldry and the members of the St. John Ambulance Brigade' for their assistance at the demonstrations and the loan of property; and Mrs. Booth for carrying out Miss Izod's duties during her absence. Miss Swinton (a class member) seconded. She did not know what they would have done with- out them. Superintendent Baldry suitably replied, con- gratulating the class on its happy results.

RECRUITING CAMPAIGN. :The Final Rally And Musical

Festival.

The final rally in connection with this week's recruiting campaign will take place to-night. Assembling at the Town Hall at half-past six, , Lord Derby's Recruiting Band will proceed to Cornhill, and the band of the 2nd Battalion South Lancs. Regt. will also proceed to Cornhill, where a meeting will be held under the' chair- manship of Sir Gilbert Greenell. Lady Greenall will also be present. The meeting over, a procession will be formed to the Town Hall, where a short meeting will be held before departing. To-morrow (Sunday) afternoon, a grand musical festival will be held in Bank Park (kindly lent by the Mayor and Corporation). Ms Worship the Mayor will be present. A particularly fine programme of music will be rendered by the recruiting band.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
LLOYD GEORGE AND THE WORKERS.

The Trade Unions Congress this week passed resolutions showing that the Labour leadeis are sincerely in support of the national policy of resisting and putting down with all practical speed the perverted idea of Prus- sianism. ,They, however, voiced some of the grievances of Labour, giving prominence to that which deals with the private profit that , is being made out of war work. Mr. LLOYD GEORGE is, we know, courageous in the ex- treme, and he, with characteristic energy, embraced an opportunity of going down to the Congress not only to take its members at their word by claiming their support for the Gov- ernment, but to tell them that the profits of employers have now been restricted in prac- tically the whole of the munition workshops, and applied to State use. In claiming the support of Labour he made it clear that the country was not doing its bet in this respect, and he made the solemn intimation, "With you, victory is assured; without ,you, our cause is lost." At a time when all available machines ought to be running night and day, a very large proportion of them are not so running, because sufficient men are not avail- able to do the work. The only solution is a mixture of unskilled and skilled labour, which means a temporary suspension of union rules. It is difficult to make some men see the advis- ability of giving up hard-won privileges, but we must go back to LLOYD GEORGEAN rock- bottom truth and remember that without the full strength of Labour behind the fighting men we cannot win. The vindication of trade union rules would, after all, be a poor ex- change for a German invasion that would make short work of our trade and the unions,

.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 11, 1915.

too. It is a matter that can be adjusted and re-adjusted when the nation is out of danger, as all the grievances can be adjusted; and Mr. LLOYD GEORGE has added one more invaluable service to his country by seizing this opportu- nity of having a straight talk with the tabour leaders, and through them with the men, upon whom the responsibility rests for victory in a conflict wh)ch has become a war between the mechanics of the Allies and the enemy. 'ln such a war Mr. LLOYD GEORGE has confidence in the superiority of his own countrymen, and so he says, unequivocably With you, victory is assured."

===== 0002601_19150911_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
MAN SHORTAGE.

Mere can be little doubt that the shortage of men skilled in the' various branches in trades that have been placed upon war work is caused, to a 'great extent, by, the absence' of men at the Front, who enlisted at a time when the cry for men was raised in the early stages of the war. The tieed for men to re- main at work is now recognised, 'and the great recruiting campaign that we have this week witnessed in Warrington has demonstrafed that very few men here are now open to the persuasive eloquence of the recruiting officer or, the allurement of his blood-stirring bands. We have sent, say, between 6,000 and 8,000 men to the Colours, the rest are engaged upon war work and' must remain upon that work; so that the margin of available men for en- listment, locally, is recognised to be small. Warrington, at all events, is doing its duty by the country in both ways—by working and fighting, and the cry of " slacker " does not apply here, except to a very small extent. If the workshops are to win the war, the War- rington workshops will be in the van to secure the victory.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
TO-MORROW IN THE PARK.

To-morrow the recruiting week will be brought to a fitting conclusion by a musical festival in the Park, at which the Mayor will be the principal speaker._ His Worship has thrown all his energy into the campaign, and, thanks largely to his personal influence and interest, the work of the week, which has been on a large and exacting scale, has run a smooth and successful course.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
THOSE PINK FORMS. Can You Help To Make A Good

Job Of Them?

VOLUNTEER EARLY.

The final stage of the National Registration has now been reached—all that remains to be done is the filling, up of the "Pink Forms.' Unfortunately there is a very serious, shortage of voluntary workers which is delaying the suc- cess and completion of the measure. The local Military Authorities confidently appeal to all— men, women, boys and girls—to devote a portion of 'their spare time to this work. Very little is asked of each one—the work is entirely clerical and entails no unpleasant duties. Surely, an appeal of this kind will meet with a generous response. To those who are too old, too young, too infirm, or are otherwise barred from .donning the glorious khaki it presents a splendid opportunity of showing, practical patriotism. It has been said that "the pen is mightier than the sword." It remains for local patriots to prove it. Only spare time is asked for. We, each and all, have some'leisure.which could not be better employed than in helping to bring ,early success to this .most necessary measure. The sooner the ." Pink Forms," are filled up and the sooner will the gaps in the ranks of our glorious regiments be filled. Do your. hit. Every little helps. Don't write. Walk into the Recruiting Office, Central Police Buildings, every time you have an hour to spare. - - - -

- Come in when you like and leave when you

The work is waiting.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
SPECIALISED MUSEUMS. Alderman Bennett Praises Warrington At The B.A.

t Alderman Bennett is representing the War- rington Society at the annual meeting of the British Association in Manchester. Speaking at a conference of delegates of Corresponding Societies on the question of Local and National Museums, he said that he should like to emphasise in the strongest possible way the remarks of a previous speaker as to the desira- bility of securing and preserving cinema films of all iraportant local and national events, which, if they could be made sufficiently per- manent, would be of lasting historical value. He also cordially agreed with Professor Geddes in his plea for a civic survey of every suitable town and district in the country. They did these things better in Germany, though, at the pre- sent time, he was almost afraid to say so. (Laughter.) The President might be interested to know that Dr. *Percival and Dr. Barnes, who were largely instrumental in forming the Manchester Literary and Philosophical Society, were born in Warrington, and that Warrington could boast the earliest free rate-supported 111118e11111 and library in the *United Kingdom, both of which were singularly rich in local relics.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
The Ideal Museum.

Their Curator, Mr. Charles Madeley, the late President of the Museums Association, in his opening address at the annual conference last year, flailed the ideal museum as a microcosm, or an epitotneof the universe. If they commenced by making each museum an epitome of the. whole life of its own locality, they could then proceed to epitomise the universe at their leisure. (Laughter.) • The Society he repre- sented, in addition to its various other objects. existed to maintain the Warrington Academy as a local museum, 'specially devote ct to the commemoration of the golden age of Warring- ton's history, and its collection was limited to books, pictures- and relics which were either directly associated with the Academy or reflected lustre on the annals of the town. He be- lieved that there was ample room in many' places for specialised local museums on these lines. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150911_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN LIBERALS.

The War Sewing-Class Makes Another Strong Start.

THRIFT SCHEME.

When the Women's Liberal War Sewing Class made a fine restart last week after their month's vacation, it was found that many mem- bers .had been working enthusiastically during the interval, and consequently a good new stock of garments and socks are already available for the soldiers.

It is interestin,, to note that a penny bank has been organise in which all may join. A large number of ladies already have realised its advantages and belong to it. Mrs. Osborne, with her well-known thoroughness and activity, is acting as hon. secretary and treasurer; and, by virtue of her position on the town Thrift

Committee, will be able to render invaluable service to the furtherance of the scheme. Oppor- tunity will be taken fully to explain the move- ment at the ward teas—which are presently to be held in aid of the war sewing-class funds. Unfortunately many women do not appear to realise that their present state of prosperity will not last after the end of the war, and therefore do not trouble to "make hay while the sun shines.".

===== 0002601_19150911_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
AT ORFORD BARRACKS.

How Men Are Encouraged To Be Keen, And

THE RESULT.

SPECIAL BY "THE EXAMINER' MAN.

If it is true, as Frederick the Great is re-

_ puted to have said, that au army 'goes on its 'telly, sand i,f it naturally follows that the better the feeding is attended to the better the army will "go,' the British Army ought to make progress in the present war, and those men who have the good fortune to be stationed at Warrington during their early days of train- ing, ought to be particularly fit. I had heard it said that the cooking arrangements and the _

feeding of the men at our local depot of the South Lancashire Regiment were the best in England, and I recently sought an opportunity of investigating the truth of the statement. I went to the barracks last Monday, un- announced and unexpected, and asked if I might see the men who have recently joined up have their dinner. Sergeant-Major Quick, who is the president of the messing com- mittee, quite willingly gave me the necessary permission, and even at that moment 400 men, hungry from their morning's exertions, had their 'faces turned • towards the Barracks Gymnasium which, in these strenuous days, has been turned into a dining hall. Inside I found a body of wounded soldiers already -seated, thoughtfully passed in before the crush. came. On a long table dinners had been placed ready to be picked up by the men as tney tiler" in and walked to their places. A SPLENDID DINNER.

Those dinners in appearance, size and quality were a splendid show. The bill of fare was : Plain stew, new potatoes, carrots and turnips, with rice puddings. What was perhaps most important of all, the food was presented in a cleanly and inviting manner. Not the odd man but paid the highest possible compliment to the cooks—the plentiful supply was eaten with a speed that would have threatened indigestion complications in ordinary civilians. • Half-way through the men were called to at- tention for a moment on the entrance of the Orderly Officer, Captain Scott. "Any complaints, men ?" he asked.

"No: sir," came the enlphatic and unanimous shout—and the serving 'of the pudding from great tins, by means of capacious ladles, pro- ceeded.

It is said that when Queen Mary visits 'mill- .. .

tary establishments she is not satisfied with witnessing what is to be seen' on the surface. . .

Her Majesty likes to see the kitchen, and it is said she has not always been pleased with the result of the inspection. Sergeant-Major Quick conducted me without any prompting round the larder and those other places which, being away from the common gaze, might not possibly be up to the .mark. Everywhere was scrupulously clean and healthy, and the store of good things —sides and sides of the best bacon, stacks of loaves, shelves full of best jams and pickles, corned beef and other articles of food —demonstrated at a glance that the Warrington depot was worthy of its reputation.

THE COOKING. But "dropping in" casually at dinner time, though it has its advantages from the investi- gator's point of view, has this disadvantage: that one has lost the chance of seeing the actual cooking. I received a cordial invitation to call again the following day and follow the whole operation. This I did, and had the pleasure of seeing the next day's dinner cooked as well as eaten. There are the best of facili- ties for cooking, baking and preparing food on the best lines, and I found everything in capital order, in, a perfect state of cleanliness and working in perfect harinony. Inquiry made as to the meals other than dinner revealed the fact that the men are on an exceptionally liberal diet.. At reveille they have milk, coffee or cocoa, at breakfast the changes are rung upon bacon, beef, sausages, bloaters, rissoles, onions, tomatoes, marmalade, bread and butter and tea. For •tea .stewed plums, corned beef, cold roast brisket, cold roast beef, stewed tripe, kippers, bacon, jara, bread and butter are in regular evidence. A substantial supper is also served free, an improvement upon peace-time customs when supper, if wanted, has to be bought at the canteen. The canteen. under the management of Mr. W. Vincent, formerly a Quartermaster-Sergeant of the South Lanca- shires, is a first-class establishment, conducted on the regimental system,'and evidently is the foundation upon which the successful feeding of the men of the depot stands. In the same block as the canteen I found the sergeants' mess, presided over by Sergeant- Major Newall, who is well known in Warring- ton; a billiard-room for the men, where tem- perance refreshments only are served out under the management of Mrs. Vincent; and a library stocked with books and papers out of the small profits made in the canteen. TRAINING COOKS.

There is a school for military cooks at Orford Barracks, which is ,turning out a hundred men every three weeks equipped for the work of going into the Army to train other cooks. At one time there was only one school of cookery in the country, and that was at Aldershot ;. now there is one in every command, and that, for the Western Command at Warrington is one of the biggest. In the ground attached to the Bar- racks these men, under permanent instructors,' at the head of whom is Sergt.-Major J. M. Brooke, erect all kinds of ovens—earth, mud, brick, and all varieties of improvisations, that the needs of the field may render necessary. One very effective oven is made of biscuit tins packed with " pog " (earth), corned beef tins being brought into use for the building of the chimntey. • Every attention is paid to the pre- vention of. waste, One of the features of every camp against which there is an outcry. A GREAT COOK.

One of the permanent instructors is a great man. He is Sergt.- Mitchell, a' standing adver- tisement to his profession, beingoalmost as broad as he is long. "Any objectiou to being specially mentioned?" said Sergt.-Major Quick to our friend, who, for seine reason, is ealled "John Bunny."

"Not at all," said Sergt. Mitchell, whose smile is as large as his physical dimensions. Put me down at 22 stone—a joke apparently as permanent as the Sergeant's position as a trainer of cooks. Yet Sergi. Mitchell must have been of' gentler proportions once, for I learnt that he was formerly in the Royal Ilorse Artil- lery, and Went through the South African War:

===== 0002601_19150911_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
KEEN AS MUSTARD.

As I *as finishing My interesting tour the Bantam Battalion of the South Lancashires, now being raised, swung into the Barracks' square from a march-out'. They certainly marched, manceuyred and handled their rifles like old hands at the game.; yet, said Sergt.- Major Quick, some ,of them had been in the .Army only a week, none of thena longer than eight weeks.

Asked for an explanation, the Sergeant-Major said they "meant business," wore keen as mus- tard, and were consequently making progress at a rate unknown in the Army in ordinary times. The care taken of, and the interest taken in, the men undoubtedly have a .great influence upon them. The dinner-time scene of plenty and content is the syrabol of the success that is being experienced at Orford.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WORKERS.

Sergt.-Major Quick, who has the oversight of the whole establishment, has been a first-class .warrant officer for 23 years, sergeant-major in the Cheshire Regiment, and garrison sergeant- major under .Sir Francis Greville at Malta, where he was recommended for a commission. He has served in the Salford police. Latterly he was engaged as inspector by Messrs. George Shaw and Co., Leigh Biewery, and on the out- break of war joined the South Lancashire Regi- ment. He is noted at the War Office as a candi- date for the Meritorious Medal. He has a highly efficient assistant in Corporal Watts, and Private Jones has charge of the larder.

Sergt.-Major Brooke, the head of the Cooks' Training School, was cook-sergeant in the Ist Battalion Grenadier Guards for 16 years. He has received one gold and. four silver medals and four diplomas from the Universal and Cookery and Food Association, London. lie was personally decorated by her fate Majesty Queen Victoria, his late Majesty, King Edward VII., and by King George V. Ile was one of the hearers for her late 'Majesty Queen Victoria, King Edward and the Duke. of Caznbridge.

REV. PERCY INESON.

Welcomed To The Bold-street

Wesleyan Church.

NEW MINISTER'S IDEALS.

The Rev. Percy meson, newly-appointed Wesleyan minister in succession to the Rev. Hugh Williamson, was publicly recognised on Tuesday evening at a meeting held in the Bold- street Church. A good attendance obtained. Mr. J. G. Withinshaw presided, and there were also on the • platform the Revs. W. Thompson Derry, 11. T. Dixon (Penketh) and J. Gunnell (supernumerary). The Rev. J. Gunnell led the prayer, after which Mr. Frank Hickman gate an organ solo in powerful style.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
THOUGHTS ON SANKEY-STREET.

The Chairman, in his address, heartily wel- comed the new minister, and said he Camp to this circuit well spoken Of by all who knew

him—and he (the '-Chairman) had known him for twenty years. The work of this circuit as compared with some was simple, and if the people would do their best was capable of almost anything: There was no doubt that it cbuld do much more than it was doing to-day: They would be pleased to hear that Mr. Inesen was deeply interested in foreign missions, and as the years rolled by the circuit would doubt- less join more and more in the growing enthusi- asm for God's work abroad. Success always

TE REV. P. IN

seemed to him one of the most ununderstand- able things one could come across; but there was no sphere of life where there were so many slackers as the spiritual sphere; and when they examined themselves and tried to sum up the work of God in their lives compared with the other interests they' should be ashamed. Mr. meson came as quite a different man from Mr. Williamson—which was a good thing; but like him he had many strong points. For one thing he would find plenty of work; he (the speaker) did not know of any c,hurch where the opporttinities were greater than at Bold-street. When one went along Sankey-street and saw the crowds of young people, especially on Sunday nights, one thought that Bold-street at least could wake up to its opportunities. They had an example in the SoitTiers' Home, that if the young people found somewhere where they could be welcome they would come. When they thought of the people who had lost loved ones, and the people who were anxious, -%eary, sad and dismal, there was such an opportunity as they had never had.

"MADE A GOOD START."

Mr. Hickman (a steward), in the course of some humorous remarks,, said they had had a sample of Mr. meson's sermons, and they were very acceptable: he had a workmanlike style and "go," and had made u good start. The people must not put their expectations too high, but should give him their heartysupport—because he would need it. He would have some play for his creative faculties—the grandest chance that any minister could hope for!

===== 0002601_19150911_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
War-Sermons.

The choir rendered the anthem "Sing a Song of Praise," after which -The Rev. W. T. Derry said they would never in that church forget Mr. Williamson; they would remember him because of what he left behind and because of what he took away. (Laughter.) Now they had chosen Mr. meson and he had chosen them, and they could depend upon it he would do his best. One might venture to prophesy that he would minister especially to the young. They were not going to influence young people by making out that the religious claims upon them were small; when young people realised that. Christ made no compromises, that He demanded every power

atid every faculty they possessed, then they would be far more likely to do well than when they expected Christ Wanted from them a limited service.

They could not be insensible to the stupendous crimes which were being committed by our enemies; they could but remember the dreadful grief in so many homes. But he thought it would be a great mistake if they were always preaching upon the war. When they were preaching saving truths they should be most earnest, or they were imposters. He had no doubt Mr. meson would seek his pAolile ;- at the same time they must seek him. • He had been three years in college and six in the ministry— that was not a bad apprenticeship.. On behalf of the ministers he gave him a most cordial welcome. • THE PASSION FOR SOULS.

The Rev. P. meson, after thanking grate- fully one and all for their welcome to his wife and himself, said there was one thing be coveted more than another—the understanding heart. He wanted them to try and feel to- wards him as they had felt towards Mr. Williamson, to feel that his heart was full of sympathy in the hour of darkness and sorrow. Ile came at time when there was a great and a grand call for ,tie church of lesus Christ. Men were being driven back as they had not been for years upon the great realities of life; and to re-estimate the values of things; they were coming to revise many things which already the Churches had squabbled over and fought for, and which were but transient. This time was a time of testing. The Church, examining itself, had come to imagine it had all sorts of ailments; but now in the great shock which had come upon them they were asking what did these great realities stand for to the young man dying away in the trenches, and to those in their midst who had been bereaved?

How We Die.

During the week ended September 4 there were 35 births in Warrington against 46 in the corresponding week last year. There were 20 deaths igainst 22 last year. Of the deaths four were from phthisis or other tubercular disease, three from pneumonia, two from diarrhcea, two from bronchitis, and one from scarlet fever. Twenty-two fresh cases of scarlet fever were reported.

Chairmanship of Insurance Committee.

At the monthly meeting of the Warrington Insurance Colnmittee, held at the Town Hall on Thursday evening, the Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock) was unanimously re-elected chairman, on the proposition of Mr. W. Bibby, seconded by Mr. Brocklehurst. Both of the gentlemen named eulogised his Worship's services during the time he had presided over the deliberations of the committee.

"A Willing Lad."

The St. Elphin's Company of the Church Lads' Brigade attended the Parish Church under the command of Sergt-Major S. C. Essenhigh, on Sunday night. when there was a memorial ser- vice for one of the lads if the company, Private B. Wainwright, 9, Willis-street, who, had died in the Infirmary after a brief illness, and had been buried at the Cemetery on the previous Monday. The Rev. T. Vickers occupied the pulpit, and referred to the fact that the de- ceased, who was only sixteen years of age, had always been a, willing lad and a keen member of the C.L.B. for over two years. The late Private Wainwright was employed as an appren- tice fitter by Messrs. E. For Shaw and Son, Ltd. His father died last Christmas.

Over 28,000 Insured Persons In The Borough.

It was reported to the Warrington Insurance Committee, at their monthly meeting on Mon- day evening that the quarterly count of the index register showing persons entitled to medical benefit on July Ist resulted in the following figures being ascertained: Deposit Contributors 415 (a decrease of 11 on last quarter's figures), Army and Navy Insurance Fund 4 (1 increase), Exempt Persons 52 (8 in- crease), Members of Approved Societies 27,347 (1,146 decrease), Married Women Voluntary Contributors 45 (9 increase); total 28,363-1,139 decrease. The Clerk intimated that, in arriv- ing at the above figures, a deduction of 1,787 had been made in respect of enlistments which had been notified by Approved Societies.

Fire At plind Factory.

Shortly after 9 a.m. on Tuesday a fire broke out in a store room on the premises of Messrs. Tilling, Gray and Co., Ltd., roller blind manu- facturers, Bewsey-street, and situate just above the premises , of Mr. B 1 Welsby, bootpiaker. and repairer. Information was at Once -.on- veyed to the Borough Fire Station, and the motor engine, and a full complement of men under the. ,charge of Superintendent Turner were soon on the scene. The firemen were, however, considerably hampered in their efforts to quell the outbreak, in consequence of dense volumes of smoke proceeding from some small wood on the upper floor. Smoke helmets had to be used in order to get at the .seat of the fire; but after about an hour's work the out- break was subdued. The cause of the outbreak is unknown, and the damage, which, happily, is but slight, is covered by insurance. The stock of Mr. B. Welsby suffered coniiqqrably through smoke and water.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
A Faithful Chorister.

A fine tribute to the late Mr. William Scotson, of Manchester-road, was! paid by the Rev. T. Vickers at the I'arish Church on Sunday even- ing. To be faithful [to the church and choir] for fifty-five years, the preacher said, showed a won- derful character; and throughout that time Wm. Scotson had, perhaps, been the most regular chorister. God asked one thing—that we should be faithful. Ile had been faithful, and was now rewarded with the Crown of Life. Quietly and dutifully he worked behind the scenes—humbly, like the Great Example and the world's great men. Because William Scotson was a humble man, he was a great man. Well might they pray for grace to follow his example.—The choir gave a most sympathetic rendition of the anthem " The Souls of the Righteous are in the Hands of God."

War-Time Economy. The Finance and Estate Committee reported to the Town Council at their meeting on Tues- day evening that at one of their recent meet- ings a letter was read from the Local Govern- ment Beard urging strict economy in every branch of expenditure, whether capital or revenue, the postponing of work the cost of which has to be borne by private individuals, unless such work be urgently necessary, and, further, requesting that the Board may be sup- plied with reports from time to time as to the nature and extent of any savings which local authorities might have been able to effect. It was resolved by the ommittee—a resolution which was endorsed by the Council—that the letter from the Board be printed and a copy sent to each member of the Council, and to the head of each of the Corporation departtaents. I • "The Labourers Few."

A good Wycliffe P.S.A. meeting on Sunday was addressed by Mr. Richards, of Stockton'lleath', who spoke upon "The harvest is plentiful, but the labourers are few." This, he said, was the root of the vision beautiful. Beauty, often un- seen, needed the touch of the human hand, which would carry out the vision as seen by the mind, and bring it to. perfection. What a field for harvests Christ could see in those around Him In the handful of disciples He saw His armies of wprkers. And they must all try to bring out the best in themselves and others !

The Chairman (Mr. B. Farrar) welcomed three new members, and also Private John Hewitt, of the Royal Fusiliers—who is now convalescent, after being wounded and gassed—and Gunner Wm. Hilson, of the Royal Field Artillery-,-on furlough. Messrs. MeEachern. and W. Beswick took part in the meeting, Mr. W. H. Payton presiding at the organ.

A PLEA FOR PRAYER.

It was on sunday evening that Mr. meson preached his first local sermon, taking for his text Col. i.. 14—" In Whom we have redemption through His blood, even the forgiveness of sins." Mr. meson, in a few preliminary words, asked his people to make a matter of special prayer the work of the church during the coming days. Many of them, he knew, already did so, and the church was very near to their hearts; still it might be that if there were others who realised that they had a share in the work, their in- terest also would be quickened. He trusted the work would prosper even more than before.

Women and Thrift.

Under the auspices of the Warrington Thrift Committee, a women's conference was held in the Council Chamber yesterday afternoon, when several addresses on the subject of how women can practise true economy were de- livered. After discussion, it was decided to publish literature dealing with the art of cheap cookery, &c., and to ascertain through the 'clergy of the different parishes to *hat extent thrift is being practised by the women of thi* town. A resolution calling on the Government to close public-houses to women was passea• Miss Broadbent presided.

Even Drugs Are Dearer !

The Finance Sub-Committee reported to the Warrington Insurance Committee at their monthly meeting on Thursday evening that et a recent meeting the Clerk submitted to thew a letter from the Commissioners intimating that a sum of £2l 18s. 11d, was available to the committee for the purpose of making ex gratis- payments to the chemists in respect of certain drugs supplied in the latter half of 1914, which had advanced in price owing to the war. The Clerk submitted a statement showing the Po" portion due to each chemist, and was author- ised to issue cheques accordingly.

Successful Warrington Baker. At the recent International Exhibition for bakers and confectioners held at London, the premier prize of a 50 guinea Challenge Cup Was' won by Messrs. Toft, of Bridge-street, Warring- ton. It is the fifth time that this enterprising firm has won the trophy, in addition to over 109 cups, medals and diplomas. Messrs. Toft bake the bread which is sent bY a generous public to Warrington men interned as prisoners of war in Germany, and the rasa letters of thanks and appreciation sent bY grateful prisoners speak volumes for the excel- lence of the goods.

Monet is much needed for the hospital at Raddos Court, and I hope that anyone willing to con- tribute will kindly give direct to me or to th• Honorary Treasurer, the Rev. G. C. Briggs."

WARRINGTON AMBULANCE MEN

At The Dardanelles.

VIVID DESCRIPTION OF DARDAN- ELLES FIGHTING.

Private John Moores, of Crosfield's Division Naval Sick Berth Reserve, and in peace times a worker in the Tablets Room, mentions the work that wiPs performed by the hospital ship on which he is serving, in the East. The letter is to his sister, Mrs. J. Powell, 9, Frederick- street, with whom he resides when at home. He has been on active 'service since the out- break of the war, and among his experiences in many parts of the world was that of being sent out to Australia—when his ship was in company with the Sydney' when she sank the Emden. He attended Latchford Wesleyan Church, is a teacher in the Sunday school, and is generally and highly respected. Previous to setting out' for the Dardanelles on board the Soudan, he served six months in the Plymouth Hospital.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
WHEN THE "FUN" BEGAN.

The lettei, dated August 17th, says : " Since I last wrote we have had an exciting and busy time in various ways. If you remem- ber we were nearing . when I finished the last letter, and it was then that the fun began. ?or several days we lay in the harbour there, expecting any. moment .to receive a load of wounded as before, but such was not the case. On Friday, August —, after patiently wait- ing, we were told that the same evening we were to leave for Gallipoli Peninsula, as. there was to be something doing. At 6.30 p.m. one hospital ship moved, out of ,the harbour, a second, a third, and so on, until it came our turn to move, and it was a grand sight in the starkness to see the many green lights and illuminated red- crosses which- adorn all' the seven hospitals as they steamed slowly in line,' with hardly a ripple on the water to disturb the stillness of the. night. Having been fore.? ,warned of what would be expected of us, we turned in early-sand were soon asleep. The next morning, instead of hanging on to the hammock till the, last stroke of. six, I, with the remain- der, was on deck at 5 a.m. The reason I will explain. It appears that) a new landing had been organised, and the British men-o'-war, who were preparing the way by pumping shells into the enemy's positions, created such a dis- turbance as to awake us all, and turn us out of our hammocks out of curiosity, to see what was happening. As far as the eye could see, there was sailing craft of every description, whilst totpedo boat destroyers skipped about like little madmen.

"At last we stopped only to find ourselves close to a British cruiser that every now and again would pour a broadside of 5 or 6 guns into the enemy. Again we moved, this time nearer to the shore, but, alas! we had not been there very long, when Mr. Turk put a shell right close to Us. This was ,enough, and we crawled a little farther away like a kitten from a hot cinder. All this time the big guns of the ships spoke death as shell after shell found its mark.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
MANY WOUNDED ARRIVE.

By this time the wounded from the field began to arrive, giving us- quite a busy time, as you will see when I tell you that the first three nights we were working till 12.0, 1.30, and the third night we worked all night. We were taking them in on one side of the ship, keeping the worst cases and shipping the others to other boats after treating them. An- other day we were still taking. in the wounded when a German Taube was seen hovering over us and hoping to make his pres- elle° felt he dropped two bombs, one on each aide of the ship, a good marksman if ho was aiming at the water, but poor if he was ,trying Lis hand at us. The same thing occurred the next day. One of. the Turkish big guns had evidently spotted a small British camp on the seashore, and proved this by handing over a few shells. However, they ended Mr. Turk, for he wasn't heard again. As the evenings came on the burning villages were a picture, as the flames rose in the air, but still the ships kept hammering away. At length, after a week of this, we moved off to a small island which was but a few hours' run, where we hoped to discharge our patients to another ship, and then return for more. We Waited there for a day and a half, but eventu- ally we sailed to with our cargo, but again we had to move, and this time to Alexandria. We left on August 12, and the next day pre- sented a new page to our already varied experi- ance. "I shall have heaps of news to tell you when I collie home that I cannot send by letter, but all good news will 'keep. We ourselves receive no news whatever, the newest paper we have seen being August Ist, so I shall wel- come the first letter and 'Examiner.' Any- how it is all for the good of the cause, so keep .en keeping on.' " Ah, well! I think I have said all that I can say at present, so I will close, still hoping to .see you soon, and hoping shortly to receive a letter.—l remain ever your lovilig brother, •JOHN."

Private k Moores encloses a 'cutting from an illustrated paper which he marks Picked up .round the Dardanelles." It is a picture show- ing a wedding at Stockton Heath between Mr. Hughes and Miss Powell, Mr. Powell's niece. The title is "A bridegroom is 'held up at Warrington on Flag Day." No names are men- tioned, but Private Moores had evidently recog- nised the parties.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
VISITS WARRINGTON.

At six ,o'clock on Sunday morning Private Moores arrived home on leave at 9, Frederick- *street, Latchford, remaining until this even- i4g. During the - visit "The Examiner" representative found him looking very well and cheerful.

At the Daganelles—said the Private—the general opiniolß amongst our troops seems to be that though We shall, of course, eventually gef through rand gain the victory, it will take us a long time to do it 'The troops againgt ,which we are fighting are practically all Turks —commanded by German officers; and the English give them every credit for being good, 'hard and fair fighters—quite different from 'their allies.

Private Moores is not the only Warringtonian on board the hospital ship. Private John Hollows, rof Padgate (who has this week paid • a similar visit home) and Private. IL Walker, •of Parker-street, are also serving thereon. 'Their principal duty is attention to the wounded, and the majority of eases are serious. The types of men are, of course, various; some take it well and others, badly—and some talk *too ranch Also they occasionally have a few 'wounded Turkish prisoners, who, of course, 'cannot talk, as they would not be understood.

'The ambiAlance men certainly have hard work and plenty of it. Their day entails a thousand and one duties of all varieties, including dom- estic ones. Nominally they commence at six o'clock in the morning; but as it is impossible to work on routine and each works in a special ward, they are sometimes called up in the small hours, or towards three—to attend, for example, to a dying soldier and relieve the "night-duty man." At times-they have even worked for 251 hours on a run, but take it quite philosophically, as they know it has got to be done.

WOUNDED.

SERGEANT STANLEY MOFFATT.

Wounded In The Head While Trench Digging.

Sergeant Stanley Moffatt, of the Territorials, has been wounded in the head. While asSisting at building a sandbag parapet he was struck by a bullet, and although the injuries were thought to be grave, he is now in hospital at Leicester doing, aLhe writes to Mr. F. Cook, 309, 'Lovely- lane, "very nicely." Company-Sergeant-Major T. S. Davies, writing to Mrs. Moffatt, 27, Walker-street, the Sergeant's mother, says :

"oth August, 1915.—1 t is at the request of your son Stanley that I am writing, this short note. We were at trench-digging last night, and he was struck by a rile shot on the top of the head. The wound appeared to me to be a rather serious one, but the doctor at the dressing station gave very good hopes of him. I saw that he had first aid rendered immediately and taken away on a stretcher to the doctor. All his words and thoughts were for you. I hope that you will be hearing good news from him in the course of a day or two."

Sergeant Moffatt is a Whiteeross employe

===== 0002601_19150911_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE JACK HOGG.

ing on July 28th, stated: "I am sorry tosay that I have some bad news for you. Your son was killed by an electric shock yesterday: The way in which it happened was as follows: He went out to work on a telephone line, which we had -laid the day before, the idea being to improve it in certain plac6. Put His Hand on Power Cable. "For some distance this line ran under' high' tension electric power lines. Your son climbed up one of the poles with the telephone wire in his mouth, obviously to put it higher up on the pole, and, not realising that the wires above him were power wires, placed his left hand on one of them. He was killed instantly, and lam thank- ful to say, according to 'the doctors opinion, quite painlessly. Although I know how futile it seems.for a stranger to say so, yet I must say that I sympathise deeply with you. I can truth- fully say that I had no, better man in my section than him. He did yhis work—aye, and he did a lot more; yet [never heard him grumble.. If he was told off to doj*.a job that was specially dis- agreeable or tiring, he \always smiled .about it. Officer's Glowing Tribute. * "He was the hardest working and, at the same time, most cheerful and willing man that I had ever to deal with. Ido not say this for the sole reason of consoling you, but because it is due to him that his parents should know that he has done his ' bit ' as well as. any man -could, or has done, to the Army. • In my opinion; it makes no difference that he was not killed in the trenches. If every Englishman had done his duty as well as your son, we should not be at war now. He died for his country as much as any• V.C. hero has. We are going to bury him to-day in the cemetery at Lillers, which has been given over to English soldiers."

A Comrade's Sympathy. Private Herbert Callaway, a comrade, wrote: "Jack. was buried last Wednesday in the ceine- tery at Lillers. He was accorded full military honours. . . . The service was read by an Army Chaplain. Everything found upon him will be forwarded-to you.,by the Army authori- ties. I extend my deepest sympathy with you in your said . Deceased was a native of Overseal, and was twenty-three years of age. He had been in War- rington for about sev4n years, during which time he made, himself exceedingly popular among a large circle of friends. He was em- ploYed here on Government electrical work at the General Post Office.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 11, 1915.

KING AND RIFLEMAN.

His Majesty's Kindly Interest In Warrington Man.

ROYAL VISIT TO HOSPITAL

Rifleman Stephen-- Mason, of the Rifle Brigade, a well-known Warrington wiredrawer, has this week been lucky enough: to have a brief conversation with Their Majesties the King and Queen. Rifleman Mason, who is the son of Mr. William Mason, 20, Napier- street, Warrington, was employed at White-. cross, where 14 served his apprenticeship under his father. He was born at " Cromwell's Barracks," in Church-street, forty-four Oars ago, but he promptly enlisted when the war broke out. He was wounded in several places slightly, but at Chapelle he was struck by shrapnel and had his foot badly damaged. He has been in hospitals at Weston-super-Mare and Bristol, and the X-rays recently re4ealed the fact that he still had shrapnel in his fle.,it and a! third operation was performed on his foot, and he is now reported to be clear of all foreign bodies. He is a well-read man, and, as will be seen by his letter, which we repro- duce practically.as sent, he ,is very good at descriptive writing. It is not given to every- one to describe as an eyewitness the visit .of a ! Monarch. Rifleman Mason is a member of the Liberal Club, and was formerly a Rugby half- back. He played for several clubs, including Warrington "A."

===== 0002601_19150911_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
VISIT OF THE KING AND QUEEN.

Rifleman Mason, writing from the 2nd Southern General Hospital, Southmead, Bristol: on the Bth inst., says : " The King and Queen visitpd this hospital yesterday. They were received by Colonel Paul Bush and other officers, the Lord Mayor, the Matron and nurses, and a number of wounded. After the usual introductions, the King, notic- ing the wounded standing, walked over to them and said, in a very kindly was, 'Sit down,. my men; sit down.' It was a pleasure to see him hold animated' conversation with several of them. /All this took place only a yard or two from my window on the ground level. The King and Queen then toured the wards, visiting No.. 1, my ward, first. So that they might speak to as many as possible, the King commenced on one side of the ward and the Queen on the other. His Majesty And A Strange. Coincidence.

"The King asked me where I was wounded. I replied : Chapelle, Armentiers, Prance,' ftnd I told him also it occuired at fire o'clock on the sth day of the sth month, 1915. He said: 'Rather a strange coincidence.' He then asked was I getting better, and I said: As well as can be expected,' and the King smiled and asked how I felt in myself; and I said: 'Very well, indeed,' adding Thanks to the splendid treatment and attention I receive.' The King again smiled and said: 'Very good,' and left me to give another comrade some cheering words. I must say I felt quite at home dur- ing our conversation.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
Her Majesty's Interest.

"In a few minutes the Queen was at my side asking me different questions,' which I answered, and on the Matron- telling \her of me coming back from Weston-super-Mare to

undergo another operation, she said how sorry she was to hear of 'that.

"A little later they visited other wards, and having done so they congratulated the Princi- pals on their good work and upon the splendid management of the hospital. They returned to where they were, first received, and, after conversations with nurses and others, entered their motor car, and, amidst ringing cheers, the King saluting, they drove away to visit another hospital. To Be Snap-Shotted.

"This afternoon I am to be snap-shotted by one of the sisters, who is going to take all those the King and Queen spoke to, and the sister is going to give me one of myself. I think, having said all I can of the Royal visit, I must close, hoping you are all in good health, and may keep so."

===== 0002601_19150911_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. JOHN AMBULANCE.

Last Night's Presentation To

Successful Students.

Last night at the Police Station the latest St. John Ambulance class received their medallions and certificates. There was a large attendance. Supf. Baldry said he was sorry Mr. W. Sey- mour, of the St. Helens cable and Rubber Com- pany, had been unable to come down to present the certificates, but Mr. J. H. C. Brooking, of the same firm, would do so in his Stead. Dr. J. S. Manson, who was cordially received, welcomed Mr. Brooking. Although 15,000 men had already been provided it was to be feared that several thousand more would be needed. (Applause.) Mr. Brooking then presented the certificates. The Proficient Members.

The following 21 passed for first-aid certifi- cates : Harold Ivan Jones, Joseph Houghton, Wm. Kilduff, Stanley C. Jones, James Price, Thomas Malone, Albert W. Howliston, Horace Blease, Wm. J. Barton, Donald Reid, Wm. Bradbury, Joseph Cartwright, Wm. Wright, Gordon B. Starkey, Albert Manssuer, Robert Wilson, William Bailey, Peter McKenzie, John C. Rees, Wm. M. Walker, James Barlow, Joseph Wilkes, Jonas Eaton and James Quinn. Two passed for vouchers—John J. Morrell and James Snailham; whilst medallions were awarded to William W. Morrison and Chas. Rees.

Of these, several are on service, and conse- quently had to send deputies. Mr. Brooking, in presenting to Dr. Manson a pocket-wallet on behalf of the students, said they were sure it was totally inadequate for the work he had done. He also presented to Superintendent Baldry a souvenir in the form of a tobacco pouch. Both recipients returned thanks. _ _ _ _

In .a short address, Mr. Brooking said ambu- lance work was one of the most unselfish pur- suits they could take up. He thanked the Officers , for their work throughout the session, (Applause.) A hearty vote of thanks was in turn accorded to Mr. Brooking.

Official news has reached Warrington this week that four local men are missing, and it

is feared drowned, as the result of the sinking of the troopship Royal Edward in the lEgean Sea a few weeks ago. The quartet all belonged to the Ist Border Regiment, and the names are: Private Harold Hackett, 196, Gorsey-lane; Private John Barnett, 180, Longford-street; Private Stanley Monks, Orford-lane, and Private Sam Irons, 31, Ripley-street.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
SOLDIERS' GRAVES OVERSEAS.

No Private Memorials Oaring The War The Secretary of the War Office announces that no crosses or memorials of any description to mark the graves of those who have died overseas can be accepted for transit. Durable wooden crosses, treated with creosote and legibly inscribed, are already in position on, or in preparation for, all known paves, and in addition all known graves are carefully regis- tered. It should be realised that numbers of graves are well within the range of hostile shell fire, which would as, effectively destroy iron as wooden crosses. The former could not rapidly be replaced, whereas the latter could be re- erected immediately approach were possible. Further, many graves are in close proximity to the enemy and can only be approached at night; therefore the weight of the cross to be erected is an important factor. These reasons, and the difficulty which would be experienced in the delivery of crosses if general permission were granted, render it necessary that, during the period of the war, only the regulation wooden crosses should be erected over graves.

PR iSONERS OF WAR. Urgent Need For More Financial Support.

As will be seen by our list of subscriptions (advertised in another column) for the prisoners of the South Lancashire Regiment, there is a falling off in the amounts received during the past week, the total reaching £3l is. 10d. This only covers the week's dis- patches, which \numbered 172. The men who received parcels this week will not again do so for 14 days. This is not very much to help them to maintain their strength, but it does go to show' that Warrington is not forgetting her obligation's to the men Who have suffered so much. A relative, in writing to' thq Parr Hall this week, sends a Card she received from one of the prisoners.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
Not Pleasant Reading.

It is not pleasant reading, but we give it to show the .urgent character of the prisoners' needs:— Have You sent that parcel of eatables yet? not, for God's: sake hurry up. Anything will do so long as I can eat it. Dry crusts, or Spratt's dog biscuits. Try your best. . . . If you were in my place you would know what it is to want."

As we intimated in our last issue, the pro- prietor of the Star•iiinema is giving the pro- ceeds of a Sunday evening performance, and we hope our readers who have not already purchased a ticket will do so, and thus ensure the success of the effort. _ _

Another prisoner, whose needs have been seen to from the Parr Hall, writes showing his appreciation, and adds:— " Have again received one of your weekly parcels. Everything was in good condition, thanks to the packer. The bread was just the sante as if it had only just left the baker, and the Oxo I have in my soup every day. They are beautiful. Thanking you again for your kindness to pie in captivity." , The fund is indebted to the Prisoners of War Fund of the ",Khaki" Magazine Co., London, for a gift of Winter's Special Fruit Bread and Special Cake, together with a quan- tity of "Nutchoc."

HAI) HIS WISH.

Vicar Of Orford And The Late Lance-Corporal Southern.

The late Lance-Corporal Win. Southern, of the Bth Seaforth Highlanders, son of Mr. Wm. Southern, Dallam, and an old scholar of Orford School, was the subject of an appreciative reference in the sermon of the Vicar of Orford (the Rev. G. Vale Owen) last Sunday evening. The hymn "Ten thousand times ten thousand" was sung, and the reverend gentleman asked his congregation to remember Wm. Southern and George Higham and Elijah Marsden, who had also passed beyond the veil. In his sermon the Vicar said: "Another of or lads has been called hence. I was talking to him a few days before he left for the Front, and 'he told me any young fellow ought to be proud to give his life for his country. He has had his wish. We must thank God he has died nobly. We shall still remember him and pray for him as heretofore."

NATIONAL SERVICE.

Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., Deprecates Controversy.

UNITY ESSENTIAL NOW.

After the opening meeting outside the Town - Hall on Monday, Mr. Harold Smith. M.P.. motored at the head of the Band of the 2nd South Lancashire Regiment to Sankey Bridges and later addressed a numerous gathering of workpeople outside Messrs. J. Crosfield and. Sons' works.

They were, the Member said, all proud of what Warrington had done, in common with other Lancashire towns, since the present tragie and devastating war began. (Applause.) It was still possible, however, to do more, and what every young man had to ask his own heart and conscience was whether he ought not—provided he be fit—to go and serve his King and country in this hour of need. They were not there to appeal to the men whose family ties were so. heavy and so responsible that they ought not to go; they. were not there

to appeal to the men beyond the proper age, nor to the men urorking in munition yards. They came to make an earnest personal appeal to every young man who ought to go and who had not yet gone.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
German Cowards.

There was 'not one citizen of Warrington-- whose heart was not filled with horror at the tragedy of this war;. who- was not filled with loathing and hatred • for the base cowards against whom the Allies were fighting—men who mutilated non-combatants and sent inno— cent women and children to their doom at the bottom of the Atlantic. There was not one. who did not loathe and detest the methods of the enemy. (Hear, hear.) There was not one of them who did not pay tribute to the glorious and undying, 4gallantry of the men Great Britain had sent, and was still going to send, to the Front. (Applause.) They Ile kei any young men who could join the ranks of our magnificent Army to do so that day, in order that they might go back to their families and say: "I have heard my country's call and volunteered my services. I did not wait until my country sent for me." The Member and National Service.

Mr. Smith went on to refer to the question of National Service., He said he had always been a subscriber to the funds of the National Service •League and was still a whole-hearted supporter of its principles, which could bit described in a nutshell as compulsion by the State, on rich and poor alike, to submit them- selves to training which would enable them to play their part in the defence of their country While reiterating his undying support of the principles of the ,League, he protested' very- strongly against the attempt which was being made in the Press, both by supporters and opponents of National Service, to arouse what could only be a bitter controversy at a time when absolute unity was essential. (Hear, hear.) By the common assent of the nation, a Government had been formed, of the best men which the country could produce, dis- regarding all party claims and regarding only ability and statesmanlike qualities. As was - well known, the Government -was composed a men who in the past had ranged themselves both in favour of and against National Service. Surely, with these men at the head of our nation, we could safely leave to them the vital decision, and accept it with confidence, and indeed with acclaniation, the moment it -was made public. (Applause.) The Voluntary System—lf Possible!

"We want, if possible," the speaker con- cluded, "to continue the voluntary system in such a manner that it will be unnecessary to use compulsion in the middle of such a war as this. I beg of Warrington to send every young man whom she can spare, in order that the great part which the town has already played may grow even greater, and that when the history of the war comes to be written, War- rington can look with pride and count by the thousand the sons she has sent to fight these great battles for her. (Applause.) I appeal to all eligible young men to join that unbreak- able Territorial Regiment, whose name will never, die in the memory of any one of us here , to-day." (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19150911_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====

THELWALL HEYS MILITARY CONVALESCENT HOME.

The following wounded soldiers arrived on Monday at the above hospital :—dunner Whinary, shell wound; Sapper John Ronald, shrapnel wound in foot; Sergeant A. Edwards, bullet wound in leg; Sergeant W. Pee, bullet wound in thigh.

•RADDON COURT POUND DAY. Another highly successful pound day was held in connection with-the Itaddon Court Red Cross Hospital on Thursday. Gifts to the value of £l6 Bs. Were received, consisting of pillows, vests, groceries, vegetables, eggs and shrimps. For these welcome gifts the committee are very grateful. Gifts of money, of course, also went to make up the total. WORK OF THE "WHITECROSS." The apizencled extremely interesting and concise account of the doings at the White- cross Hospital, since its opening as a healing- house for soldiers, was submitted to Wednes- day's .meeting of the Warrington Board of G uardiaxis • We have, so far, had in the Whitecross Hospital 142 patients, of whom only 54 now remain. So high a proportion of satisfactory discharges is very gratifying and reflects great credit on the medical and nursing staff. Special praise is clUe to Dr. liodgkinson and Dr. Jagger. who have performed several difficult operations with complete success, and to Miss Emerton for the splendid manner in which she has dis- charged the duties of matron; her example has been an inspiration 'to her assistants, whilst her sympathetic temperament, bringing help and encouragement even to the severest cases, • has done as much as, or perhaps more than, her professional skill to win for her the whole- hearted admiration of her patients. The Committee of Management highly ap- preciate the , loyal service of the professional nurses on the staff and also the splendid help rendered by the voluntary workers who have served as unpaid probationers. Many offers of help in this way have been received; but it has ' not been practicable to make use of all, -since - the number of patients has never exceeded two- thirds, and is now barely a quarter of what could be accommodated. There have been many difficulties in connec- tion with the administration; some, discredit- able to those who raised them, May be dis- missed with silent contempt; others, arising from military patients being placed under civilian control, from the natural irritability of invalids and convalescents; and even from the exuberant joy of speedy and unexpected re- covery.of health, have given the committee great anxiety. In dealing with all these matters, where there semed: any conflict in the wording of different parts of tlie official regulations, we have subordinated the letter which killeth to the spirit which giveth life; and interpreted all other rules in accordance with the golden phrase in the 25th paragraph: "Patients should be made as happy and comfortable as possible." We have felt that, in acting up to this ideal, we were giving effect to the unanimous wish of the Guardians and discharging loyally the duty entrusted to us.

COAL FOR THE POOR.

Government Suggestion To

Rural Council.

At the fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council at Dutton on Tuesday, Mr. T. Antwis presided. A circular letter was read from the Local Government Board stating that, owing to the number of miners who had joined the Colours, there was a probability that the supply of coal for home consumption would be less than usual and it was important that efforts should be made to accumulate- stocks, The Price of Coal (Limitation) Act now regulated the price of coal at the pit-head, and the cost of the hire of private railway waggons, but this might not prevent the retail price being a serious burden on the poorer classes. In London an arrangement had been made as to the sum to be charged over the cost of coal delivered at the station or siding by the merchants to cover the expenses of distribution and profit, and the Board asked the Council to consider whether a similar arrangement could not be made with the merchants in the Council's district.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====

An explanation of this was asked for, and it was requested that the Rural Council should replace the fountain and re-open the well, which were public institutions and conveniences. • .Ir,
