Distinguished Visitor.—Amongst the casual visitors to Lymm on Sunday could be noticed the Lord Mayor of Manchester.

Red Cross Concert.—We understand that, in aid of the Red Cross Hospital, i a concert is to be given by the Savoy Party from Manchester, in the Primitive Methodist Schools on Monday,

Old Gentlemen Pass Away.—At the Parish Church on August 12th and 18th respectively were conducted the burials of Frederick Simp- son (62, of Mobberley) and Thomas Johnson Kinsey (79).

Red Cross Nursing.—ln the list of First-aid Certificate winners, Daresbury Division Red Cross Society, read at the Warrington Town Hall on Saturday afternoon, was the name of Miss Goodier, of Bridgewater-street, Lyifim.

Bazaar At Statham.—This afternoon, with the object of assisting the Lymm Red Cross work; there is to be held in the Statham Schools a small bazaar organised by the scholars and helped by Miss Dalton, one of the teachers. It commences at 2.30.

Two Marriages.—ln ;the September Parish Magazine are recorded the marriages of John Leather (Rostherne) to Priscilla Knight (Lymm) on August 2nd; and William Henry Miller to Margaretta Tipping (both of Lymm) on August 14th.

Private James Holt! —Mrs. R. Wibberley, of Booth's Hill, paid on Monday a visit to Liver- pool Northern Hospital to see her wounded brother, Private James Holt. She found him very cheerful and his left arm is making good progress. He hopes to get home to Lymm before very long.

Late Corporal George Appleton.—Private Percy Clare, of the 1/5 Cheshires—now with the 3rd Section Royal Engineers—writes: "We were very sorry indeed to hear the sad news about George. It mpst be very hard for you people at home—more so than it is for us. It upset us very much; we could hardly realise it for a while with seeing so many of the same cases out here. It is very sad, and he was doing so well."

British voluntaries.—Mr. Wm. Cooke will during this .month play at the Parish Church voluntaries by British composers: Idylls (Ed. Elgar), Solemn March (A. H. Edwards), Andante Grazioso (Jas. F. Fricker), Impromptu (J. W. Elliott), Introduction and Allegro (L. Henni- ker), Meditation (A. C. Keeton), Melody (C. Hubert IT:Parry), Menuetto—lmpromptu (Har- old A. JebOult), Elevation (Wmi• Faulk), and Berceuse (T. W. Hanforth).

Sergeant Thomas W. Field.—On Wednesday afternoon—among new patients at "Brook- field," the Lymm Red Cross Hospital—arrived Platoon-Sergeant Thomas W. Field, of the 1/7th Lancashire Fusiliers, whose home is at New- fields. The Sergeant, who is looking very well despite his many Dardanelles experiences, was at first conveyed to the Hope-street Military Hospital. On Thursday afternoon, by the courtesy of the Matron, Sister Colburn, "The Examiner" man had long conversation with him.

Private J. Booth.—Private James Booth, of the Ist Welsh Regiment, has been paying avisit home to Cross Brow, Statham, this week. On leave from the 'Front, he arrived on Tuesday. morning and has left early this morning. He is looking fine, and thinks the fighting is going on .splendidly for Us. He has been in the thick of it since January, participating; among other things, in several terrible bayonet charges. He goes straight back to the Front. His brother, .Lance-Corporal John H. Booth, of the same regiment, is at present in hospital at Boulogne, suffering from rheumatism.

Cheshire Prisoners of War.—The Rector writes: "Lieut.-Col. Hamersley is trying to ar- range for the ' adoption ' of men of the Cheshire Regiment who are prisoners of war in Germany. Lymm is asked to find four or six people willing to undertake this post for the same number of prisoners. The names and address of the men will be supplied, and the work consists in send- ing a parcel once a fortnight. Any willing to offer are asked to send their names either to the Rector or to Mr. F. E. Evans. Packets of 111 b. and under may be sent post free through the Post Office by parcels post, and those over 111 b. post free to the Prisoners of War Help Committee. Full particulars will be given on application."

Mr. Stidson Broadbent.—ln the Parish Church on Sunday week were held the quarterly meet- ings of the Church Men's Society. "The after- noon meeting," writes the Rector,- "will be chiefly memorable for the farewell the members took of the honorary secretary. Mr. Broadbent had not been secretary many months, but with his organising ability he had managed to infuse fresh life into the society, even during that short time. Mr. Broadbent has now removed to Cos- terton, near Kirkby Lonsdale, a most interest- ing parish in Westmorland, where he will employ himself in evangelistic work. We shall probably have another opportunity next month of referring to Mr. Broadbent's work at Statham. After very careful consideration Mr. J. T. Milnes was appointed honorary secretary in succession to Mr. Broadbent, Mr. R. Oddie and Mr a Hodgson promising to assist him in the work."

Gladness 'Mid Sadness.

"I am tempted to quote from a sentence from a letter received yesterday from one of our old boys, a member of the R.A.M.C. After telling of the arduous and terrible work in which his corps was engaged he speaks of five men who had died of their wounds during the previous week, and adds, 'Over each of them we held a beauti- ful service, and in my spare time I made a small cross for each one, so that any passer-by might" know where they lay.' These simple words are eloquent of the spirit of our men. May God bless them and keep them, and bring them back safely to their homes. "There must, I fear, be the usual sad con- cluding paragraph to my letter. Two more of our lads have given up their lives to their country during the past _month. George Appleton and Edwards Gilberts, these make six or seven from our Pepper-street. School list of one hundred and fifty-eight old boys who have joined the • Colours. Our heartfelt sym- pathy goes out to the poor parents, Mr. and Mrs. Thomas Appleton, of Statham, and Mr. and Mrs. Edwin Gilberts, of Newfield. On the .other. hand our congratulations are due to Private Harry Brooks, late of West Hyde, for the award to him of the Russian .Medal of Si. George, for his many brave acts as stretcher- bearer in the sth Cheshires."

LYMM " LADS " ABROAD.

The Rector's Appreciation Of Their ‘Vork And Spirit,

In the September issue of the Parish Maga- zine, the Rector (Rev. G. B. Thurston) writes " 1 am taking the "opportunity this month of writing what 1 know you would wish me to write, an appreciation of the work and spirit of our Lymm lads in France, Flanders and at the Dardanelles. . . . It has been my privilege during the past few months to have received many letters and messages from abroad, and nothing gives me greater pleasure than their arrival, and I always try and answer them by return of post; if this letter should come into the hands of any, of .our men abroad, may I make it the medium for a message to say how glad I shall be to have a line or two from them at any time?

===== 0002601_19150911_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
A Fine Percentage.

"The general impression left upon the -mind from the many reports received is that Lymm has every reason to be proud of her young men, and in the first place because of the large num- ber that have willingly offered themselves. I .do not know -what the experience was of other enumerators for the National Register; I can only speak of the district which was committed to me. In that district the proportion of females to males was nearly two to one, and there was hardly a name out of two hundred of Which it could be said, 'He ought to join the Army.' That is certainly a most.praisewortliy record. • Another thing that has been very marked has ben the high excellence of the letters that have appeared in the Press and that have been received privately. They are just what letters should be—modest and inform- ing, and withal,. written in simple, easy language, bringing With them, above all things, the personalities. -of the writers. It speaks well for our elementary education when it can claim such a high standard of letter-writing among its past pupils, as in fact it has been justified in all ways during this present war.

" Determined To Persevere."

"It does one good to read of the courage and unselfishness of so many among whom it has been one's privilege to live; it heartens one in what is at times somewhat monotonous and difficult work; but we in Lymm have had a full reward during the last few months. The little acts of kindness. and consideration have been manifold; there have been frequent records of the care and thought of the com- missioned and non-commissioned officers for their 'men. When any untoward event has be- fallen there have been letters of true sympathy written by comrades in the field to stricken relatives at home. Wounds ,have been made little of, and the spirit of determination to persevere amid great hardships and privations , has beat universally-manifest. It is impossiblg

NEV MINISTER.

Lymm And Bucklow Hill Congrega-

tionalists' Decision.

At a meeting of the Lymm Congregational Church members it has been unanimously decided by a full attendance of deacons and members to give a pastorate-call to the Rev. S. Gill, at present of the Stibbings Congrega- tional Church, Essex. Mr. Gill, who has 'occu- pied that pastorate for seven years, has, dur- ing that time, never missed a single Sunday there, and is highly spoken of by other ministers in that district.

Should he accept, the reverend gentleman will reside at BuCklow Hill—also having charge of the church there. He is the son-in- law of Dr. Sgurrah, who is a retired minister and a doctor of divinity.

Dr. Scurrah conducted the Lymm services on Sunday, and also hopes to occupy the pulpit at the harvest festival to be celebrated on October 3rd. The following day—Monday—he will give in the school a lecture, entitled "A Measured Man."

LYMIYI TERRITORIALS.

Private Percy Clare, now engaged on mining work with Private Joseph Clare, writes: "You say that wants to know when I am going to get a wound and come home. I am quite satisfied as I am; I don't want any as long as I keep pulling through, and don't mind what I go through until the end. I am sorry to say I did not get the parcel or cigarettes; but I ex- pect some of our lads got them, and as long as they did I don't mind."

To his mother, Mrs. Davies, at Booth's Hill, he writes :

"We are not allowed to tell you much about our job yet. We have eight hours tip in the trenches and twenty-four away. While we have been here we have met a young man named P. Clare, and he has brothers the same names as my relations in Stockport. . . He comes from Yorkshire; we seem to know his face."

Private Hen 7 Brookes, in a letter received on Thursday by his mother, at 1, West Hyde, says:—

"I think Jim Gilberts will be coming home in the course of a day or two, as they have taken full particulars from him: . .. Well, we are having a few days' rest just now, al- though things are nothing so keen here as what they were up in Belgium."

Private Jim Taylor, who has remained up to now in the Lincoln Military Hospital and who has not yet recovered the use of his left arm after being wounded, now writes home to 1, West Hyde : "I am in the best of health. I have—as much as I can learn—got discharge; my. dischare; I was before the Board last ° Sunday, and °they put me down as permanently unfit for further duty. So I shall be home for good in about eight days, with a small pension, as the arm is no better for movement, although I have no pain with it. . . I hope you will explain to Mr. and Mrs. Gilberts, that I am sorry I can- not write them a -sympathising letter, as I cannot put into words how I feel the loss of Ted: he was my best pal." On Thursday afternoon Private Jim Gilberts arrived home at 16, Newfields, on eight days' leave.

On Friday of last week Private J. Gilberts wrote home :

"I am; very sorry to say I cannot get to the service fat Lymm Church, in memory, of his dead brother], which I would very much have liked to attend. But our Captain, Mr, Hartley, has been talking to me to-day, and he' had just received a letter from home asking him to do his best to get me home for Sunday; but it is impossible. He is doing his utmost to get me home on leave as soon as possible; so cheer up, for I shall be sure to come over before next week-end. . . . We are out of the .trenches for a rest. . . ." •

PRIVATE 11. CADMAN.

To England After Some Weeks' Serious Illness.

Private Henry Cadman, a Lymm Territorial, at present belonging to the Mining Section of the Royal Engineers, has this week written to say that for some week o he has been in hospital seriously ill, but is now "on the mend," and expects shortly to be in England. He did not write earlier because he was afraid it -would " worry " his relatives and friends.

It will be rethembered that in April Private Cadman was wounded in the arm by a bullet which 'shattered his rifle whilst with the "Terriers," but on that occasion did not cross to England.

Aged 21, he is in peace times employed as a bricksettor by Mr. J. Holt, and lives on Booth's- hill, attending the Congregational Church and Sunday School.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE WALTER RADDEN.

Bullet Passed Over Bead And Under • Feet:

To his wife at Park-view, Statham, Private Walter Haddon writes after being wounded for the second time:—

"I am in hospital at a place called Etaples, close to the seaside, and I am, not up 'to the mark. But lam very _lucky to be living . . We went out on the Thursday niorning at 5.30 on a post in a wood, and we hadn't been in the wood above an hour when twelve Germans opened fire on us; they, had been lying down waiting for us to come into the wood. So We started to fire back at/ them. We were only fifteen yards off them, so you can tell what sort of a/putting-up we had. The worst of it is there Were only three of us to twelve of them; and we did very well to keep them at bay. Then when poor Teddy Gilbert i got shot it only left tvo of us;.he got shot right through the head, aird dropped on my legs with his head. I turned round and lifted his head up. but it was no use; he was dead, and I feel very sorry for his parents. . . "Shan't Get To England."

"Well, I got my wound .in the ankle, but am very glad to tell you that the bullet didn't touch the 'bone. It went right through the flesh part, and another bullet went right through the sole of my right boot, but didn't , touch my foot. I shohld likd you to see the boot. I don't think I shall get to England this time; but I don't like the idea .of going back to the regiment a third time—tis they say the third time pays for all. It has fairly shaken me up, the bullets going over my head and under my legs: you can tell how lucky I woe! 11/4)1

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 11, 1915. VOLUNTEERS AT CIIELFORD. IUNIONS AND RECRUITING.

Lym.rn Platoon Participates in

Mimic Battle.

At Asti° Park, Chelford, on Saturday after- noon, the Manchester district companies of the Cheshire Volunteer Regiment provided a mimic battle as a contribution to a programme in aid of the funds of the local branch of the Red Cross Society. Colonel Dixon threw open the grounds and the gardens to the public, and among the other attractions were a shooting gallery, military bands; theatrical performances, a collection of war relics, and a sale of work and produce. Between three and four thousand people were present. Amongst the others present was the Altrinc- ham Battalion. This includes the Lymm volun- teers, who turned out in good force under• the command of Captain Williamson • and Sergeant Lister-Farrar. Leaving • the Drill Hall early in the afternoon, their own transport cars conveyed them to Chelford in less than an hour. The Battle Explained. The general idea Of the battle in which the local men formed part of the attacking forces centred in the defence of a bridge which, held by a Macclesfield contingent, led to a road from Crewe into Manchester. The defenders came into conflict with the outposts of an enemy line extending from Northwich andZhelford,to Bux- ton and Macclesfield, and as the attacking force was much larger and a mounted brigade section of it took the defenders in the Tear, the issue of the fight did not seem in doubt. No official de- cision, however, was given._

The march past before Colonel Dixon followed, and much enthusiasm was displayed over the 2,400 men in the ranks, mostly old Volunteers now past military age. After being complimented by the officers and many spectators, the Lymm men returned by transport, reaching home at about 8 o'clock.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
SERVICE IN FRANCE!

There is a probability that the Lymm Volun- teers will be affected by the War Office having accepted (in principle) an offer made by the Central Association Volunteer Training Corps to raise battalions for certain special services in France. The terms upofl which this work will be undertaken have not yet been settled, although the senior officers held a private meet- ing to discuss the matter. The Volunteer Asso- ciation includes more than 1,000 corps, repre- senting .a bout 350,000 men.

The Curate-in-Charge.—The Rev. E. E. Cham- berlain, who has acted as curate-in-charge of Padgate since the Vicar undertook the duties of Army Chaplain, has been appointed curate-in- charge of Tamerton Foliot. Devonshire. His work is referred to in this month's Padgate Parish Magazine as follows "Mr. Chamberlain has for thb past twelve months Worked the greatest energy and devotion in our midst, and we may say he has worked the parish sec:and to none of his pre- decessors. There is scarcely a corner in this scattered parish which has not received a visit from him, and those who know him hold him in the highest esteem. By the sick and the whole Mr.-Chamberlain will be.greatly missed, but we are certain he leaves a lasting impression for good behind him. When fie came to I'ad- gate he was quite a stranger, but during his stay he has, made a great circle of friends, and his future career will be carefully watched by them. With regard to Mrs. and Miss Chamber- lain, their severance from the parish will be keenly felt by many. They are ever willing and anxious to do a kindly act and to speak a cheery word." The Rev., J. Hirst. the Diocesan Secretary of the Church of England Temperance Society, will succeed Mr. Chamberlain..

===== 0002601_19150911_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
Trades Council Will Not Support Military Week.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
DIFFER FROM CONGRESS.

As is well known, at the Trade Union. Con- gress this .week a resolution was carried recording approval of the action of the Parlia- mentary Labour party, in co-operation with other political parties, in the national recruit- ing _ campaign. _ _ _

The oi:galiisers of Lord Derby's West Lanca- shire Recruiting Campaign have in all towns met the various representative bodies and trades organisations with a view to discussing recruiting methods. This arrangement—as previously stated in " The Examiner "—has been found to work very §ptisfactorily; and in all other towns the co-operation of the trade unions has been gained perfectly simply. At a Warrington Trades and Labour dinner held on April 27th—it is interesting to note— Mr. F. Berry (the chairman) said one could not help thinking about the 'national crisis and. of their trade unionists who were fighting their battles out in France in a struggle which would doubtless be a prolonged one. "It makes our hearts bleed," he continued, "to think of the terrible agonies which many of our members are going through, and our activities have been crippled by the absence of those members in the regrettable conflict. But those of us who have not yet felt the nation's call shall at least stand by the principles to which we 'have adhered in the past—that our members who have gone away shall not come' back to find lost the principles for which they fought so hard." (Loud applause.) Some difficulty appears to have arisen in the town in connection with the Trades and Labour Council's attitude towards ,Military Week.' The following correspondence took place recently :

===== 0002601_19150911_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
CO-OPERATION SOUGHT.

On August 28th, Captain 11. M. Mathams, commanding the administrative centre, 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment, sent the following letter to Mr. Charles Dukes, secretary of the Trades and Labour Council:—

"Dear Sir,—As you are doubtless aware, Lord Derby's Recruiting Campaign reaches Warring- ton on the 6th September, when it is hoped, in the course of a week's activity and propaganda, to attract a large number of recruits. On behalf of the committee which is "condutting that cam- paign, and also on behalf of the 4th South Lan- cashire Regiment, for which it is hoped to en- list the. recruits, I am desired to ask you for your co-operation, which I am sure you will gladly render. "Lord Derby's committee is anxious that representatives of the West Lancashire Terri- torial Force Association should meet the mem- bers of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council one evening during the week of tbe campaign, with a view to consulting them as to the effect of recruiting on labour generally, and as to the value or otherwise of the methods which have been and are being adopted to secure recruits. "In other towns and centres visited in Lanca- shire, the Territorial Force AssoCiation has had the benefit of the counsel and advice of the Trades and Labour Councils, and feels that much valuable information and help has been secured irr this way. Doubtless the Warring- ton Trades and Labour Council• will he willing to meet the representatives of the Territorial Force Association in a similar manner, in which case I. should be grateful if you would write to me, signifying your willingness that this should be done, and also suggesting the time and day of that week which would be most convenient to the Trades Council.

"On hearing from you I shall be glad to make definite and final arrangements.

"Thanking you in anticipation, I am, yours faithfully, R. M. MATHAMS, Captain, command- ing Administrative Centre, 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment." "DO NOT FEEL CALLED UPON."

The following reply was sent on September Ist : -•••

_ "Dear Sir,—My Council B.C. have considered your communication of August 28th. I am in- structed to say the Council do not -feel called upon to take any action in the matter of re- cruiting.—Yours sincerely, for CHAS. DUKES, Secretary, 11. N."

"OUR COMMON PATRIOTIC PURPOSE."

Captain Mathams sent the following letter to Mr. Dukes:—

"Dear Sir,—l am obliged by your letter of September Ist, 1915, in reply to mine of the 28th August, in which you state that your Coun- cil E.C. do not feel called upon. to take any action in the matter of recruiting. "This is a decision which .must be regretted, especially as my letter did not ask specifically that the Trades Council should do more than meet representatives of the Territorial Force Association In order that they might hold joint counsel as to the value of the methods which have been, or are being, adoiited to secure recruits, and as to the effect of recruiting on labour generally. In theory at any rate,. it would seem that no body had a better right to be consulted upon questions such as these than the Trades and Labour Council, which, by a refusal to meet those responsible for the present Recruiting Campaign, deprives itself of the right to criticise action which may, in its opinion, be :of grave moment to Labour inter- ests.

"May I ask you whether the decision you have conveyed to me cannot be reconsidered, and in doing so may I point out that the Territorial Force Association would welcome any fair criticism which the Trades Council might care to forward, provided it is in accordance with our common patriotic purpose—to secure the maximum of national efficiency with a minimum of individual injustice? "Furthermore, may I repeat the request con- veyed in my letter that you personally will give us your assistance in the forthcoming cam- paign.—Yours faithfully, it. M. MATHAMS, Cap- tain."

===== 0002601_19150911_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
"WE HAVE NOTHING TO ADD."

Mr. Dukes replied September 4th : "Dear Sir,—Yours of the 2nd inst. to hand. My E.C. met last night, and after careful con- sideration they instruct me to' say we have noth- ing to add to our communication of September lst.—Yours sincerely, on behalf of the E.C., CHAS. DUKES."

===== 0002601_19150911_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADE UNIONS CONGRESS CONDEMN CONSCRIPTION.

The Trade Unions Congress has at Bristol unanimously adopted the Parliamentary Com- mittee's resolution condemning the conscription campaign and declaring belief in the adequacy of the voluntary system.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADE UNIONISTS SUPPORT THE WAR.

By an almost.,olM vote the Trade Unions Con- gress, speaking in the name of nearly 3,000,000 trade unionists of Great Britain, declared its belief in the justice of the Allied cause and pledged itself to support the Government to the end. The terms of the declaration were as follows :

"That this Congress, while expressing its opposition—in accordance with its previously expressed opinion—to all systems of militarism as a danger to human progress, considers the present action of Great Britain and her Allies as completely justified, and expresses its horror at the atrocities which have been committed by the German and Austrian military authorities, And the callous, brutal, and unnecessary sacri- fice of the lives of non-combatants, including women and children; and hereby pledges itself to assist the Government as far as possible in the successful prosecution of the war. On. the policy of participating in the war the discussion revealed no difference of opinion. The statethent of Mr. Sexton that it was for Great Britain a, war of defence—to prevent, as he put it, a Charleroi•at Bristol and a Louvain at Ox- ford—went unchallenged to the end of the de- bate' The only conflict of opinion disclosed by the debate was upon the question at what stage overtures for peace should be made or heard. On this point the feeling of the Congress was overwhelmingly against the small party of idealists led by Mr. Ramsay Macdonald.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
Cost Of _A _Misunderstanding

On The Road.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
COLLISION AT "DOG AND DART."

Coroner Brighouse conducted an inquest at the Police Station on Wednesday on the body of Joseph James, of 15, Haydock-street, Earles- town, up till the time of his death a foreman cabinet maker in the employ of Messrs. G. and J. Weaver, of Bridge-street, Warrington, who was killed in a motor accident near the Dog and Dart Inn, Grappenhall, on Sunday, August 22nd. On that day, at about eleven in the morning, Mr. James was driving a motor cycle and sidecar along the Knutsford-road, Grappenhall. His wife was in the sidecar, and his daughter sitting on the carrier. As the machine was turning into the Lymm-road, the intention being to go to Manchester, it came into collision with a motor cycle ridden by an Altrincham man named Rustage, who was accompanied, on another cycle, bi a friend named Oldham, from the same town. As a result of the collision Mr. James was taken to the Warrington Infirmary, where he died the same evening. An inquest was called for the following Wednesday, but, owing to the in- disposition of one of the Altrincham men, an adjournment was announced, after the evidence of identification had been taken. THE WIDOW'S AND DAUGHTER'S EVIDENCE.

Mrs. James stated, on Wednesday morning, she, .her husband and daughter were going to Manchester on the morning of the accident. Approaching the Dog and Dart corner on their right side, they were on the point of turning into the Lymm-road, when witness noticed two motor cyclists coming in the opposite direction, and immediately afterwards one of the bicycles crashed into them. Both motors Were over- turned. Her husband had driven a motor cycle for over three years, and had never met with an accident before.

In reply to Mr. Thomas Co6per, who appeared for the widow, Mrs. James Aid that her hus- band knew the country quite well, and there was no reason for the existence in his mind of any uncertainty as to which way to go. They were going to Manchester with a specific object, and deceased would not have wasted time by taking a roundabout route. Miss Edith James, in her account of the accident, said that her father kept his machine close to the footpatfi whilst going round the corner.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
GOING TO SOUTHPORT.

Arthur Rustage, electrician, of Altrincham, said tliat on the morning of the accident he and his companion were on the way to Southport. He was slowing up to stop at the Dog and Dart, when he noticed a motor cycle, with a lady in a sidecar and another on the carrier, approach- ing from the direction of the bridge. The machine was on its proper side of the road, but suddenly it swerved and came across the road. Mr. James gave witness the impression that he was going straight across into the Knutsford- road. Turning suddenly into the Lymm-road as he did, the accident was unavoidable. The road at that point was 28ft. 9in. wide, so there was plenty of room. Witness had been driving a motor cycle about twelve months. Cross-questioned by Mr. gooper, witness said he was not travelling faster than about four or five miles an hour when the accident hap- pened.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
ACCIDENT UNAVOIDABLE.

Frei.lk. Oldham, the last witness's companion on the occasion of the accident, gave an account of the affair that bore out Rustage's evidence. He, too, gathered from Mr. James' manner cf negotiating the curve that he was going straight on along the Knutsford-road. The accident was unavoidable. Witness and his companion were only travelling at six miles an hour at the outside. They had both shut off their engines on the top of the bridge. Mr. James was making at least three times as much ground as they were.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0187.txt | included=true | included =====
AN EYE WITNESS'S ACCOUNT.

George Blease, of Grappenhall, said that on the Sunday morning in question he was walk- ing.' along the Knutsford-road, Grappenhall, from Latchfo'rd, in the direction of the Dog and Dart, when a motor bicycle, with sidecar attached, overtook him, travelling about eighteen miles an hour. He was about fifty yards from the inn when the vehicle passed him. Then he noticed two more motor cyclists coming down the Lymm-road. As they arrived at the corner they seemed to think that the other cyclist intended to go straight on along the Knutsford-road, for he swerved out as if to do so. They were travelling very slowly. The accident seemed unavoidable.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
CORROBORATION.

John Charles Dickson, who was walking with the last witness at the time of the accident, corroborated Blease's evidence, and estimated the,, speed of the two cyclists at ten miles an hour, and Mr. James' at about fifteen or six- teen.

The local Superintendent of the Cheshire County Constabulary, stated that, from all in- quiries he had caused to be made, he had come to the conclusion that the accident was unavoid- able.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0189.txt | included=true | included =====
CONFLICTING EVIDENCE.

Miss Blease, a witness called by Mr. Cooper, gave evidence that conflicted with that already given, for she stated that, whilst she was stand- ing at the Dog and Dart corner, opposite the inn, she saw the accident and what preceded it, and it seemed to her quite clear that Mr. James was turning- to go towards Lymm. He kept near the footpath till th 6 collision occurred.

The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death," expressed the opinion that the accident was unavoidable and nobody was to blame, and voiced their sympathy with the widow and daughter. Mr. Cooper, on behalf of Mrs. and Miss James, acknowledged the expression of sym- pathy, and stated that his instructions, had merely been to protect the good name of Mr. James.

The Coroner said that he had been very favourably impressed by the way Mrs. James and her daughter had given their evidence.

A FATAL JOKE!

Mate Who Came To Tell Yarns And Was Drowned.

An inquest was held by Coroner Brighouse at Warrington Police Station on Wednesday, on the body of a nineteen-year-old youth named William Law, of 3, Pitt-street, Widnes, mate of the flat Cayman, which is owned by the Bishop's Wharf Carrying Company, of War- rington. Briefly the circumstances of the young mate's untimely death were as follows: About 10.15 on Saturday morning, Law was leaning against a rail on board the barge Sankey, moored at Atherton's Quay, Warrington. He was engaged in conversation with another man. to whom he offered threepence to clean his barge. The Sankey's captain, coming up from the hold, playfully grabbed at the money, which Law had in his palm, and the rail suddenly gave way, precipitating both men into the water. The Sankey's captain was rescued, but the youthful mate of the Cayman was drowned. The body was recovered abcajt an hour afterwards. Mr. Frederick Hodson watched the inquiry on behalf of the Dock Labourers' union.

THE TORPEDO.

Sir,—No doubt many of your readers are aware of the fact that the late Robert White- head was the inventor of the now famous Whitehead Torpedo, the potentialities of which have been forced on the world latterly. It will be of interest to know that Robert White- head was a Lancashire man, having been born in Bolton, in 1823, and educated at the Gram- mar School in that town.

It may be worthy of note and of interest to you to know that My late father, the Rev. William Thorp, 8.D., for inany years Rector of Byrne Intrinseca, Dorset, who possessed great mechanical ingenuity, having built two organs in his spare time himself, was also a native of Bolton, and a bosom friend and fellow-student with the late Robert Whitehead at the same Grammar School. My father often told .me of the 'many interesting and happy hours they spent together in modelling boats and making toys of a mechanical and ingenious nature, which eventually led up, in 1866, to the invention of the Whitehead Torpedo, which has been the means of causing such a terrible loss of life to combatants and non-combatants alike in this great war.

The late Robert Whitehead, during his life- time, received many honours from several of the crowned heads of Europe and other coun- tries, and eventually died in. 1905, his old friend, my father, having predeceased him in 1904.

IN THE COURTS.

LOMAX-YARD SCENE.

Husband Charged With Wounding His Wife.

Joseph Marratt, of 2, Lomax-yard, Warring- ton, was charged at the Borough Court on Monday, before the Mayor (in the chair), Aldermen Pierpoint, Smethurst, Jas. Evans and Bolton, Councillor Wilkinson, and Messrs. W. Furness, S. Wright, G. Pilling and W. Roberts, with assaulting and wounding his wife, Victoria Marratt, with a pint pot, at 2, Lomax- yard, on the 4th, and also with assaulting, in a similar way, Mary Ann Bromley, at the same time and place. Superintendent Williamson said that the police desired a seven days' remand, as one of the women assaulted was not yet in a state to give evidence. The Prisoner went home on the evening in question in a drunken condition and in a bad temper, and, without the slightest provocation, struck his wife on the head with a pint pot, knocking her down, The other woman went to assist her and was similarly treated ,by prisoner, who was eventually separ- ated from his wife by another neighbour. The police were called in, and the man was arrested and first-aid was rendered by the officers to the injured women, in whose heads stitches had to be inserted.

The remand asked for by the police was granted.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
Two Months For Failure To Pa:‘

Maintenance Allowance.

Edward John Lunny, musician and, lion tamer, of no settled abode, was charged at the Borough Court on Tuesday, with non-payment of arrears of his wife's maintenance allowance, which, with costs, amounted to ..tl7 9s. 6d. The prosecutrix, Mary Clayton Lunny, resides at 108 a, Bridge-street. Councillor Roberts was in the chair, and had as a colleague Mr. C. Jackson Holmes.

The Chief Constable said that the 'order in respect of which the cltirge was brought was made out in August, 1913. It was for 15s. per week. The prisoner, from the first, had made no efforts to meet his obligations. The police had had a great deal of trouble in ascertaining the whereabouts of prisoner, who had boasted that the police were not capable of ,finding him. Prisoner denied this, and said be would pay hack all the money he owed.

The Chief Constable said that the prisoner was only making excuses, and he really intended to go away either to America or Australia. He had made no effort to fulfil his cibligations. _ _

The prisoner said he was quite willing to look after his wife. -

A list of offences against the prisoner was giveniby the Chief Constable, who stated that once or twice he had been to prison for failure to pay arrears. . The Chairman said the Bench we're now going to give prisoner a chance of starting a clean sheet. He would go to prison for two months.

A SAILOR'S "FRIEND."

Man With Bad Record 'Who Denied Bad Intentions.

John Whitehead, alias Thomas Boyd, 'hailing from King's Cross, London, was charged on remand at the Borough Court on Thursday, with travelling on the London' and North- Western Railway between London and War- rington without a ticket on Wednesday last week. He was first brought up on Thursday of last week, but was then remanded for seven days in order that the police might .make inquiries as to his identity and record. These, when revealed by the Chief Constable, on Thursday morning, were not of a favour- able nature. Just before the 11.45 p.m. train— due at Warrington at 3.15 a.m.—left Euston, prisoner was discovered by officials. apparently asleep in a third-class compartment, the only Other occupant of which was a sailor. The officials were unable to shake prisoner out of the stupor into which he seemed to have fallen, and the train went on its way. A message was sent to Warrington, asking the officials there to deal with the matter, and 'When he arrived there prisoner was apparently in the same condition as when he left London. In fact he had been asleep ever since, except when he was telling lies about himself. The nolice had discovered his identity by means of his finger-prints. He was' first committed at Middlesbrough in 1879, and since then had had a large number of convictions, chiefly for theft. His record was a shocking one. There was no doubt he was going to rob the sailor in the train. Prisoner: That sailor is a friend of mine. Ile was going to the North of Scotland.' I can give you his name. , The Chief Constable: Well, then, he's not particular who he has for friends. -

A fine of 405., with the option or a month's imprisonment, was inflicted by the Bench, Which was composed of Mr. A. J. Jolley (in the chair), Mr. W. T. Williams, and Dr. Naden.

FARM AND GARDEN.

TREATMENT FOR BARB WIRE INJURIES. After a barb wire accident, do not apply axle grease or any rancid ointment to . the wound. Wash the wound thoroughly with warm water. Then clip the hair as close as possible around it, and wash again to remove any hair or other foreign substance. When sand or hair has lodged in the depths of a deep cut, never use a wet sponge to clean it out. use a piece of absorbent cotton or cotton batting. There will be much less risk of infection, it will be use- less to put stitches in a wound that is very deep Or ragged, or that is located in a place not in perfect rest when the animal is making natural movements. Dry dusting powder has a healing effect on barb wire wounds and other large, moist cuts and abrasions. Such a powder may be prepared cheaply by mixing together equal parts of slaked lime, sulphur, and charcoal. Prevent lockjaw dangers in nail wounds of the hoof by opening them up freely, which pro- vides drainage for serum and pus. Saturate with a solution of corrosive' sublimate and water in the proportion of 1 to 500. Cover with dusting powder, absorbent cotton and a bandage. This treatment should be repeated daily until the wound is healed.—Dr. A. S. Alexander, Department of Horse Breeding, Wisconsin.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0202.txt | included=true | included =====
THE USE OF BRACKEN.

One source of organic manure which is not, ,we think, made 'nearly as ;much use of as might be by growers whose lot is cast anywhere with- in a reasonable distance of commons, wastes or woods in which the material grows in plenty— we refer to the common bracken fern, Pteris aquilina. There is, says the "Fruit Grower," no material which can be used as bedding to animals that is capable of providing • a better source of organic manure, it has the power of absorbing the elements of fertility voided by animals, and when itself it decays in the soil it provides, as we have said, an excellent form Of organic matter: Yet one sees_ year after year that this valuable source of soil fertility is to a very great extent 'neglected. All who keep cows, pigs or horses, in fact,, any animal. should do their very best this season to collect and stack dry all they can get of this form of litter. Remember that all kinds of littery manure will become scarcer and dearer as time goes on.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0203.txt | included=true | included =====
EXTRAORDINARY PRICES FOR HAY.

At the present time the best ryegrass and clover hay, this season's growth, is realising almost fabulous prices at the Liverpool Market, quotations on Tuesday being as high as Bs. 6d. per cwt., or £8 10s. per ton. It must be several decades since hay sold at such a figure either at Liverpool or elsewhere, and the subject is Creating the greatest interest in local agricul- tural circles. In looking at these prices, however, it must be remembered by farmers who generally sell their hay on rail that hay carted and sold to the Liverpool Market must be cut and trussed by the grower, which is not the case where it is bought in the stack by hay dealers, who do all this wbrk themselves, the seller only being expected to cart the hay to the station. But even allowing 10s. or 15s. per ton for the cutting and carting, the price (writes the agricultural correspondent of the " Ormskirk Advertiser," August 27th) is still an abnormal one, and is somewhat difficult to account for. At the moment there does not appear to be a shortage. True, first crops were not heavy, but the second crops almost every- where are excellent, and should make up quite an avergge yield for ryegrass , and clover. Apparently a panic has seized some of the cow- keepers and teamowners of Liverpool. Curiously enough, in the greater portion of the Ormskirk district, where hay is sold by the lump to the dealers, trade is absolutely at a standstill in consequence of the at- titude s of 'the Army authorities in connection with the matter. Dealers state that they are not allowed to load clover and ryegrass hay at any of the railway stations without a permit, and consequently they are unable to do business with the farmer or grower, even though the latter may be willing to sell. On the other hand, meadow hay can be freely bought and sold, and commands £5 and £5 ss. per ton in the stack. -

===== 0002601_19150911_art0204.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WINTER MILK SUPPLY.

The secretary of the Dairy Trade Protection Association, referring to a report of a proposed increase in the price of milk, stated, on the 30th of August, according to the "Times," that at the present time farmers are facing the winter with even better prospects than any pre- vious years, and there is very little likelihood of such prices as have been suggested. There is now an abundance of milk, and its market value for purposes such as cheese-making and condensing has fallen rapidly. On the other hand, the " Telegraph " asserts that the price of milk on a twelve months' con- tract is likely to be, fixed at is. 2d. to is. 4d. per, imperial gallon wholesale, so that the cost retail will probably be 6d. a quart during the winter.

HONESTY.

Few subjects useful for winter .iecoration are more easily obtained than the seed ncds of the common honesty. The plant is raised from seed. When the pods are required for decoration they should be taken singly be- tween the finger and thumb and gently rubbed. The outer scales then fall off, leaving the iDner and almost transparent division intact. The white flowered variety produces the best pieces, and only those seed branches that are strong and well ripened should be selected.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0207.txt | included=true | included =====
THE VICTORIA PLUM.

This is again maintaining its reputation as the surest cropping plum, and those, that have only. a small space Should make a note of it for planting .this s autumn: Of course,. for flavour it cannot be compared , with the greengage or others of the lovely purple plums; but as the Victoria is mostly used for cooking it is always a stand-by. For the kitchen there is no better variety, the fruit being Urge and the stone very small. When allowed to get fully ripe the Victoria is not to be despised for dessert •

AUTUMN MANURING OF STRAWBERRIES It is often supposed that it is best to allow strawberry beds to remain untouched after they hay& been trimmed over at the end of the season, the decaying leaves, weeds, and all litter being carefully removed, and a clean sitrface maintained until a mulch is applied in the early spring to support the crop of fruit in the coming year. This is I think a mistake, as from my experience I find it of great assistance to loosen the surface, which has become quite hard from the constant movement of the pickers, and apply a mulch of well-rotted manure, which will benefit the plants while they are forming their crowns and prepare them for supporting the coming crop of fruit. All fruit growers are agreed that success can only be obtained by the treatment meted out to the plants 'durigg the previous autumn.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0208.txt | included=true | included =====
PERPETUAL SPINACH.

This is an excellent and useful vegetible that can be had ail the year if sowings are made at three different times—one in April, one in July, and the third during the present month. Often in very hot, dry weather the summer spinach is liable to run to seed, therefore, there aro but few leaves, and these of very poor quality. The perpetual spinach, on the contrary, grows well in hot weather,' and by feeding the plants one can during the summer and autumn obtain a good supply of leaves. The sowing.in July must be made early in the month so as to give the plants time to become well established before the winter. The plants from the September sowing come in during the early spring, as this -.spinach is quite hardy, and often when the ordinary prickly seeded spinach is cut off' sur- vive 9 the winter.—Wick, in "Farm and Home."

With a little practice a man can get Izs despondent over the state of his shoes as he ought to be over the state of his soul.

WORKS' AMATEUR ASSOCIATION.

Runaway Win In Individual Merit Competition.

The holdership for the ensuing twelve months of the "Major Crosfield" Shield—the trophy offered in connection with the annual Merit Competition promoted by the Works' Amateur Bowling Aseociation—was decided on Saturday, when, in 'the presence of a number of interested spectators, the finalists—A. Gleave (Crosfield's) ,and H. Benion tWhitecross) took the green. The contest proved to be a sur- prisingly one-sided affair, Gleave having a runaway win, making his total before his oppo- nent had scored at all. The winner played a masterly game all through and enhanced his reputatiOn, as a first-rate trundler.

LIVERPOOL AND DISTRICT

ASSOCIATION.

Championship Cup Competition.

EARLESTOWN VIADUCT WIN THE FINAL. Before a crowd of about 703 spectators, Earles- town Viaduct Institute defeated Stanley Arms at Rainhill, on Saturday. The game was a very close one, and the Earlestown club was only successful by 'five points. The winners well deserved their success, for they played a sound game throughout. Is It Worth It?

"To stop the advance of baldness," says a physician, "improve the general tone and vigour. Take plenty ,of outdoor exercise, sleep eight hours a night, do not indulge in tea, coffee, liquors; do not worry and do not over- work. Above all, keep a cheerful mind." Say, doctor, isn't that a pretty stiff price to pay for a little hair?

CRICKET.

RYLAN DS'S RECS. v. WESTDALE cLiverpocl and District League Champions).

This match was played at Gorsey-lane on Saturday, before a large attendance. Westdale Were fully represented, whereas the home team were minus several of their regular players. A keen game resulted, Westdale wanting three runs to save the game when Taylor (captain) 'joined Jordon, wh2) had batted throughout the innings. By steady play they eventually won the game by two runs. C. Waddell took six wickets for 18 runs and F. Roberts seven wickets for 14 runs and were the most success- ful bowlers; whilst Captain A. Renwick (16) and J. W. Jordon (10 not out) were the only players to reach double figures. Scores:— Rylands's Recs.—F. Lemon h Waddell 2, F. Roberts b Taylor 2, F. RYLANDS'S RECS. SEASON. A Very Fine Record With Bat

And Ball.

The visit of the Westdale C.C. to Gorsey-lane concluded what has been a very eventful season for the Rylande's Recreation C.C., as the fol- lowing record will show :—Played, 16; won, 11; lost, 4; drawn, 1; rtrirs for, 1,069; runs against, 704.. INFANT MORTALITY.

Statistical Returns Are " Dis- tinctly Alarming."

The " Lancet" says :—" Recent statistical returns of the births 'and deaths of infants in

this country are disquieting. Regarded in- dividually they are bad enough, but taken col- lectively they are distinctly alarming." After dealing with the causes of the continued fall in the birth rate, the writer says:— "Inquiry at certain important infant welfare centres in London wmild seem to suggest that the many social and domestic factors of dis- turbance connected with mobilisation; with the distribution of bounties, and with partings and meetings, have dislocated the even tenor of many a family's daily regime.' "In fact, without imputing any more serious possibility, it may safely be said that the con- ditions have not been favourable to sober domestic life and the pursuit of those essentials of mother craft which are so intimately associated Nirith a low infant mortality..

.

"So serious does .the position appear to those who are actively engaged in combating deaths among infants that the National Association for the Prevention 'of Infant Mortality have ar- ranged for a._ conference at the Mansion House on October vith, at which the Lord Mayor has consented to preside, and at which the causes that are responsible for the present state of affairs will be fully discussed by many of the foremost authorities on the subject."

===== 0002601_19150911_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEM I.ER 11, 1915.

STORIES FROM THE TRENCHESi

ORFORD LADS

Relate Their Experiences To

Their Vicar.

This month's Orford Parish Magazine is specially interesting, as usual, by reason of the many letters which the Rev. G. Vale Owen has received from Orford men, who appear to be stationed at every point in this world-wide war. Most of the writers say how delighted they .are to get the Magazine, which informs them of the whereabouts and doings of many of those they have known in civil life. We give several extracts :

===== 0002601_19150911_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====
PITYING THE TURKS. Man Who Was Wounded In 32nd Action.

." As I lie in bed," says a gunner in the King's Navy, who has been operating in the vicinity of Gallipoli, but is now in hospital wounded, "I think of those poor devils, the Turks, who have fallen victims to the shells I have sent at them from my six-inch gun. It as the old saying goes, all fair in love and war. I should like to get hold of the Turk who laid me hors de combat. I'd give him some- thing for nothing, I do bet. It was my thirty- second time in action when I got hit; so I have not-done so bad." Turks Mutilate—But Are Better Than Germans.

"There is one thing, the Turks are a little more civilised than the Germans, although they were always considered wild. But they show no mercy for our wounded; they have been seen to probe our dead two or three times with their bayonets to make sure they were dead. And our wounded have had their tongues cut out and ears cut off by the Turks, as I have seen myself."

===== 0002601_19150911_art0235.txt | included=true | included =====
TERRITORIALS' CHARGE.

"Not Haling A Very Bad Time At Present. 7

"It is now a month since our battalion have done duty in the trenches, as we were am pointed Divisional Pioneer Battalion, and our work consists in digging the trenches and taking material up to them. Whilst on this work, I am sorry to say, we have had one killed and several wounded. Half the battalion are billeted close to the firing-line, and they do duty close 'up; and the other half are a few miles. behind, where they do the work of forti- fying, &c. They change over every week, and the two companies—or half-battalion—who are out, work until dinner-time and then amuse themselves in playing cricket, football, boxing or other games. Altogether we are not having a very had time at present. Our billets con- sist of all kinds of improvised shelters, some with holes dug in the ground and with oil- sheets over the top to keep the rain off; others have walls of sand-bags with all kinds of coverings—canvas, tar Pauling, oil-sheets and bags—to help keep the draught out. Things in general round this particular part are very quiet except for an occasional shell or two."

" Quiet Times."

Nearly all the letters from Flanders, says the Vicar of Orford, report quiet times during the past month. To quote from one letter: "You would hardly think that there was a war on at times. Games of cricket, football, &c., and the Germans put half-a-dozen shells over us to let us know they are still living. I picked up about a dozen pieces round about where I was sitting at dinner."

===== 0002601_19150911_art0236.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
NEAR SHAVES.

-

===== 0002601_19150911_art0237.txt | included=true | included =====
Escapes That Appear ".Nothing But A

I'.liracle."

"To me," writes one of the Vicar's corre- spovidents, "it is simply marvellous what near shaVes you have, and come out without so much as a pin-scratch. But when you are in it you don't think about that or anything else, but just getting done what there is for you to do and getting out of it as soon as 'possible. I give every one of these fellows the highest praise, for they keep as cool and collected as if there was nothing up. They are well-liked all round here by their own comrades, and it has been said they have done as good a work as any Territorial Regiment out here."

===== 0002601_19150911_art0238.txt | included=true | included =====
Faith In God.

"I have read the authentic account of the Angels at Mons, and, without a doubt, this war has made Englishmen realise and put their whole frost and faith in God, because there are times in the trenches, and even coming out of them, when nothing but a miracle will save you."

===== 0002601_19150911_art0239.txt | included=true | included =====
HEAVY ENEMY LOSSES.

"Our Artillery Blew Them Up hi All

Directions."

"I see by this morning's paper that a lot of my old friends have gone to their last resting- place; 71 killed and a long list of wounded.. That comes by holding trenches. We went into those trenches on July 7th—those taken by the Rifle Brigade—without any loss, but, like true Lancashire. lads, we stuck to the trenches and the Germans knew who were in before two days were up. They made four counter-attacks on the 7th, and were repulsed each time. I must not forget our Artillery. They did splendid work. One counter-attack they were Watching them form up for it, and as soon as they moved to attack our Artillery blew them up in allidirections—the air Was full of German legs and arms. Their losses must have been terrible compared with ours, for when our regiment went into the trenches on the Tuesday night they .could not get in for dead Germans. They had to lift them and throw them over the top, and they were up to their ankles in blood. So you ,can judge for yourself what it was like. But the ,best of the affair was they got short of ammunition,' so they found a stock of the ,Germans', and rifles, so they gave them a dose of their own, and that has paid them back for Whit-Monday. But they did not half bombard our lads. They almost knpcked the trenches level With the ground; and still they stuck it till they were relieved on the night of the Bth."

===== 0002601_19150911_art0240.txt | included=true | included =====
HIS "OLD 'SCHOOL."

Lancer's Letter To His Former School • Teacher.

Mr. S. W. Jeffery, headmaster of Wycliffe :Day School, has handed us the following letter which he has received from Horace J. Smith, now serving in the 16th Lancers, a former scholar at Wycliffe. Mr. Jeffery thinks the letter does much to contradict the statements we sometimes hear that the elementary schools are not now turning out such good writers and intelligent men as those turned out a few years ago. "I hope," says the writer, "you will excuse the. liberty I am taking in writing to you. I was in Warrington on leave from the trenches at Ypres in January, and I was informed that you had placed a shield in the old school with the pames of the old boys who were serving their King and country. It must have quite a lot of names on the roll now; I have met quite a lot of my old' schoolmates at one time or another since this trouble began, and also oUr old rivals—Heathside boys., I walked up to the school 011 my last day at home, with the intention of seeing yourself and the shield, but my courage was nob equal to the task._ I did xiOt mind facing a few Germans, but my heart failed me when I saw two or three of the lady teachers that were instructing the young mind in the intricacies of English grammar, &c. Happy Memories—And Painful Ones.

"I often think of the many happy days I spent in Wycliffe, also a few painful memories ' cross my mind; one of the latter stands out in bold relief, and it is closely connected with your name. I was climbing up the spout for a ball that was on the chapel roof, when someone said Look out! Here's Jeff.' I let go and volplaned down. I am enclosing a letter I received from another old Wycliffite, who also has affectionate memories of the days gone by. We little thought in those happy (lays that we should be taking part in the struggle for exist. ente and honour that is now going on. Well, We have got to see it through now, and I am sure that any boy who passed through your hands will not be wanting when the time

comes to show the German brutes what Eng- land is made of. If "There Cannot Be Peace Till

"I cannot describe some of the horrible sights we encountered on the fight from Mons and our k subsequent advance to the Aisne and Ypres. There cannot be eace for the world whilst such inhuman brutes as the Kaiser's hordes are capable of again doing such deeds, and it is our duty to smash them until. they cannot rise to be a menace to life and liberty again.

conclusion: may I give a message to the boys and girls who have to follow in our foot- steps?—that they will strive to be Worthy of the great sacrifices that are being made in order that they may live in enjoyment of the glorious liberty and' justice of the British Empire. "My best wishes to you, sir, and all the staff of Wycliffe."

===== 0002601_19150911_art0241.txt | included=true | included =====
HOSPITAL SIGHTS.

H Slay-at-Homes Could Only See Them.

Private William Bennett, of 75, PierPoint- street, -23 years of age, an employe at Bewsey Forge, and now serving in the MA.M.C. at the Dardanelles, writing to his father, Mr. John Bennett, voices the general complaint from his part of the world that letters are not reaching him.

"Please don't worry yourself over me," he says. "We are pretty safe for the time being. During the past week we have been attached to a stationary hospital run by Australians. . . , God knows how any man could stay behind if he could but take a peep inside and see what these poor fellows have received in doing their duty. It is in a hoSpital ward that one gets a good idea of a man's character. Here are men from all parts of the world—all Britishers who have done their little bit in the true sense of the word. Some are cripples for a time, others are maimed for life; yet it is hardly possible to find amongst these men one who is not 'eager to have another go at these black dogs of Turks

===== 0002601_19150911_art0242.txt | included=true | included =====
How To Receive "News."

"I have news that. will be good for J. Mannion's family. One I know was talking to him on Friday, the Bth. He said .John was quite well and fit up to then. "Whatever news you receive, do your best and receive it bravely, be it good or bad. Any man ought to be proud to be allowed to.. come out here We are getting along very well. The foosi is fine and there is nothing in the shape of necessaries that we are in need of now. . . . I have' jut had news of our Ernest. lie came out of action on Sunday and is still fit and well."

Ernest had been reported killed in error it fortunately transpired.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0243.txt | included=true | included =====
BAPTISM OF FIRE

For Warringtonians Before They Landed.

Private J. Shaw, signaller in the Inniskilling Fusiliers, writing_ for himself and his chum, Private A. Harris, also a, signaller (both belong- ing to Warrington and being with the Mediter- ranean force), says:— " Since we enlisted we have received the old ' Examiner ' each week, and found it most in- teresting to read letters sent to you by 'Tommy' at the Front. We left England on July 12th, and made a new landing here on August 7th, and I can tell you it was a most awful baptism. We were shelled before we set foot on the beach and had several wounded. But there was no stopping. We threw off our packs and on we went using the rocks along the beach as shelter from shrapnel. After running for a consider- able time we came to open country, and I can tell you we had a warm time of it. A Good Point About Shrapnel.

"Shrapnel and rifle shot were whizzing past Us just like rain. One good point about shrap- nel, it lets you know when it is coming. It sings as it comes along, and 'immediately you hear it down you go full length :on the floor 4 ?ace downwards. •It has also a nasty way of hitting you in several places at the same time. By midnight we had driven the Turks back and taken about three miles of land, and also cap- tured a small hill, but the price we paid for it was severe. When I and my chum look back on that day of landing we think it A. miracle how we pulled through, and not one man of us would like to go through the'same again. We are now at the base, for a well-earned rest, but are ex- pecting any minute to go .back to the trenches. "The weather here is very bad. It is terrible hot during the day time, and at night it is per- ishing cold. We are hoping to be at home and the war over by Christmas."

===== 0002601_19150911_art0244.txt | included=true | included =====
LIKE HEROES.

Warrinuton Men In A Hot Corner Of Flanders.

Private A. Clancy, B Co. 10th Lancashire Fusiliers, *rites : " I would be very pleased if you would insert the following letter in your valuable paper, as I see that you have been publishing letters from soldiers in other regiments out here. I .think that the people of Warrington would like to know what their townies are doing in the 10th Lancashire Fusiliers, in which there is several Warringtonians. "Well, we are 'somewhere in Flanders,' and in a pretty hot corner at that, and I must .say that I admire the way the lads have behaved under fire.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0245.txt | included=true | included =====
Detected By Germans.

-" To be under fire to me is no new experience, as being an old soldier I was expected to do what I could to help them through. One night I was detailed with several others to draw rations, and it was a risky job, for we had to go across open ground for about a mile before we could get any cover from the Germans' fire. Well, the Germans were sending their star- lights up as usual, and they show the ground up like daylight. They spotted us when We had got .about 400 yards to get clear. Well the bullets Came around us in thousands and the lads were beginning to lose their heads, and were for run- ning it, when I thought that if I did not take control of the affair it would prove fatal to us, So I looked around and saw some old shell holes and 1 shouted 'come on, lads, into these coal. box holes' and so down we went, and gave the Germans the idea that they had sent us all West, and so fooled them very nicely. After some waiting we crawled the remainder of the journey. The boys thanked me in no half- hearted manner when they realised the real position they, had been in; so I told them that presence of mind was a good asset to avert dan- ger in many cases._

7 "We lost a good, brave chum when we lost Private P. Collins, but hdlikvas not in the same company as 'myself, but still we saw quite a lot of him and were very sorry about it.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0246.txt | included=true | included =====
A Credit to the Town.

"I might mention one night in 'particular, when we had a real bad time of it. I was on listening patrol and got out close to .the Ger- mans' barbed wire and I spotted one repairing ' the barbed wire. I was getting quite close to intending to take him prisoner if possible, but he saw me and did not fancy a hand to hand scrap, for he doubled back and disappeared. Well, they started to rapid fire at me, and how I got away I don't know. Well, everything went all right for about three hours after this, and then they started in earnest. Rifle grenades, machine guns, bombs and 'coal-boxes' began to fly around our trenches, and the boys' knew noy what real warfare meant, for it was terrible. I was pleased the way the boys stuck to their posts. Private Curley, , who was 'knocked down by the concussion of a.' coal-box' ('Jack Johnson '), Private Lucock, Private Hannan, Private Finnity, Private Sharples, also Sergeant Spenser behaved like heroes, and came out without a scratch, hut we had several casualties. But if the lads of Warrington be- have like they did on that night they will be a credit to the old town. I was proud of them. I assure you"

are depicted. Only one Mormon appears, and even then he is only a shadowy figure, but indeed sufficient to pervade the play and cause all the trouble and adventures through which its characters pass. There is a distinct plot, which tells a charming little love story. The fact that the chief comedy part was played in London by Mr. Edmund Payne is sufficient to show that the humour is by no means neglected. Indeed "The Girl from Utah" is one of the funniest musical comedies presented for some time past. As presented by Eric Burton Company, which .will visit the Royal Court Theatre next Monday, it is beautifully mounted and gorgeously dressed. The company is an exceptionally strong one, and includes the following well-known artistes: Mr.. Harry Phydora, Mr. H. V. Cropton, Mr. Charles Ross, Miss Tiffie -Premman, Miss Maude Thorne, and Miss Ruth Trafford.

There will be two performances nightly and a matinee on Monday.

MR. WILL A. JACKSON

Honoured By Bradford Army

Veterans.

Mr. Will A. Jackson, the popular manager of the Warrington Hippodrome, held until 'his appointment here in May, the position of manager of the Bradford Palace Theatre. Whilst in that town, however, he also did much other work, and of this one part was the founding of the Bradford and District Army Veterans' Association. His interest and services have now been recognised, for on Thursday of last week Mr. Jackson was presented at Bradford with a beautifully illuminated address—work of one of the members, Mr. Levi Swift, aged 72. At the ceremony, which was attended by a number of the veterans; Councillor A. Ayrton presided, and remarked upon the Pleasure it afforded him to be associated with this pre- sentation on behalf of the Army Veterans. In calling upon Mr. Swift to hand over the address, he remarked that its execution had been on his part a labour of love. .

In a neat little speech, Mr. Levi Swift then expressed the gratitude of all the veterans to Mr. JabltSon for the )vork he had done for them. He hoped he would meet with every success in his new place in Lancashire. (Applause.)

Mi. Jacklion suitably replied, remarking that the gift was one which he would continually cherish and be proud of. The Veterans' Association would, always .remain one of his most cherished memories. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150911_art0248.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ADDRESS.

The handsome address—which now hangs in the circle lounge at the Warrington Hippo- drome—is endorsed by mauy members of the Association, including seven whose ages total 563 years, and who fought in the Crimea and the Indian Mutiny. It says:-- To William Alfred Jackson, Esq. Dear Sir,— Information has reached us which has caused pro- found sorrow to this little community. We are grieved to know that arrangements are being made for the. transfer of your professional services to a distant town—recognising as we do that largely through your efforts and magnificent display of public spirit the above Association was inaugurated in Bradford. Our long experience on life's journey enables us to realise that notwithstanding the num- berless demands on your time, and the high pres- sure existing in all professional pursuits to-day, you have found time to render a splendid service in the amelioration of the lot of these aged servants of the State. In the rush of life, as we now know it, every day brings a crop of anxious cares, important duties and serious thoughts; therefore the British public may perhaps be excused for partially forgetting such names as Balaklava. and Inkermann—which, like Cawnpore, Delhi, etc., have been relegated to the schoolbooks of our grandsons' children. To your distinguished honour be • it recorded, Sir, coupled with a sma,ll band of worthy citizens, a great amount of comfort has come to those who have left their 70th milestone a long way in the rear, and who "did their bit" some threescore years ago in the, historic campaigns above men- tioned. In the multitude of friends who .are wish- ing you God-speed, we beg you, Sir, to believe that none have a greater desire for your future well- doing and happiness, than the humble individuals who have ventured here to address you. Framed by Nature to preside over the distribution of whole- some amusement, your popularity in Bradford has become a household word; you have enlisted under your banner that section of the people who dwelt in the ranks of aimlessness and despondency; you have drawn them from undeairable environments in their spare hOurs to dwell for a period in an atmosphere where we find it is a joy to live. The halo of geniality and good humour is concentrated in your being; sadness cannot exist in the air you breathe, and those fortunate people who have the good luck i to become your companions are duly infected thereby. Ours is an acute personal loss. We hope that the noble example you have shown in Bradford will constitute a precedent for the emula- tion of others, by sympathetically maintaining the hest interests of your profession.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0249.txt | included=true | included =====
CLEVER COMEDY SKETCH AT THE HIPPODROME.

What is probably the cleverest and most amusing sketch er those that have been acted at Warrington for some time is that entitled "His Rest Day," presented at the Hippodrome this week by Matthew Boulton and Co. Those who can appreciate a genuinely artistic bit of comedy stage-craft; with quite a Houghtonian flavour, should, not miss this delightful little effort. The Pedersen Bros., comedy aerial artistes, give a sensational turn. An element of humour helps to make their performance one of the best of the kind that Warrington has seen. "Father Time," a comedy duologue, by Minnie Hunt and Rhys Thomas, is an amus- ing item. Irazo plays beautifully on the violin, and also very cunningly in the way/ of imita- tion of other musical instruments. The sing- ing of the Four BaChelors is most harmonious and immensely enjoyable. Tom B. Barr, comedian, and Royde and Villiers, vocalists and patter comedians, contribute worthily to the excellence of the, programme, and there are, of course, the pictures, which never fail to interest.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0250.txt | included=true | included =====
Impersonation Next Week.

The programme during the forthcoming week k especially strong, and includes: Signor Arturo Bernardi, the great Italian protean artiste; under the royal patronage; Mason and Bart, novelty gymnasts; Lillie Soutter, comedienne; Billy French, dancing comedian; Alec. Shaw, Scotch whistling shepherd and mimic; Fredrica's Whirlwind Terriers, the talk of the towns; Little Dorothy Dagmar, Juvenile comedienne; and the customary war- pictures.

===== 0002601_19150911_art0251.txt | included=true | included =====
SPECULATION AND SPECTACLE AT THE KINEMA.

To-day's particularly good programmes at the handsomely redecorated Star Kinema are topped by "Fanchon the Cricket," a thrilling drama in which Mary Pickford appears. During Monday. • Tuesday and Wednesday attractive fare-bills included : "The Pit," a powerful-plotted spectacular story of specula- tion in the great world-wheat markets of Chicago and the love affairs of a great mer- chant engaged therein: particularly realistic -were the scenes swayed by the plamouring mob of frenzied financiers. "Patty's Magic Pants" was a hilarious " :Keystone" full of fun- and frolic. An •absorbing item was "The Living Dead," which abounded in eerie and dramatic scenes. "Snow Sports" afforded much instruc- tion and amusement, incidentally covering some beautiful scenery. " Pathe's Animated Gazette" came out in strong edition full of war-news both at home and abroad, and including the Lancastrian section.

Warrington Soldier-Policeman Honoured By Czar.

Mr. Martin Nicholls, chief constable of War- rington, has received a letter wing that Police-Con.stable Walter W. Cooke—a member of. the ;Borough Police Force, at present on leave from the Front—has been awarded by the Czar of Russia the Cross Of the Order of St. George. The following is the War Office communica- tion,_which first announced the honour :--

His Imperial Majesty the Emperor of Russia has been graciously pleased to confer, with the approval of his Majesty the King, the Cross of the Order of St. George, 4th class, to No. 2506 Corporal Walter W. Cooke. P.C. Cooke, whose "gallantry and distin- guished service" thus brings such credit not only to the Police Force but also to the 'town, is 24 years of age. On the war outbreak he was called up to rejoin the 2nd Life Guards—his old regiment; and left for the Continent after training on Salisbury Plain. Shortly after- wards he was' granted leave to come back to England and get married. Later he returned to the firing-line, where he has remained until his present .respite.

It is one thing to.inarry for love and quite another thing to carry out the scheme

MONEY TALKS.--No. 2. How shall I begin to Save? OWNINAVAIAvwxS INSISHMER2 MINSWISIENCIM q THE question to the ordinary, Man in the Street, lie who is in receipt of a small weekly wage, ,is not so easily answered as some people think. It is not difficult, by any means, to tell others how to do it, but to do it one's self—there's the rub. That is why Thrift has been called a Virtue, doubtless. fjj THERE are so many things needed in the home—small things may be, but they all take money. And the increased prices of the necessaries of life, which are needed day-by-day, make saving harder than ever. And the children—bless them—their appetites seem to grow with the prices.' And the father or mother who neglects them so as to save is an impossibility. q SPEAKING of children reminds me that there is a Penny Bank at the Day School. And whilst I cannot set aside 'shillings for myself, yet I can save pennies for them. I remember seeing a placard on the walls to the effect that a penny-a-day saved meant thirty Shillings a year. I never quite looked at it in that way. I'll start them on Monday in earnest. And when they've saved a pound they'll be able to transfer it to the Savings Bank, in BUTTERMARKET- STREET, and receive a half-penny a month interest. It's worth a trial, anyway. The Runcorn Office is at 40, High-street. „ =ammulommingumummi 14421ffl Autumn Fashions. 'CAL INTELLIGENCE. mmisonsamots AMERICAN AND "THE EXAMINER." What A Discerning Cousin Thinks About Us. A Warringtonian, who had forwarded to s friend in America a copy of "The Examiner containing an account of the historic charge of the local "Terriers" on June 16th, received the following reply:— " Thanks for your paper. I must say my sympathy goes to all who sorrow. I was very much interested in your little weekly and candidly confess that, while I have read the majority of Lancashire weeklies ' from time to time, I cannot remember reading one which contained so much news of a good local character as the one you sent me. It should, I imagine, have a good circulation."'

He is 26 years of age, single, an old scholar of Heathside, and a member of St. Paul's Church. He has been a Territorial for six years, and re- joined on the outbreak of the war. PRIVATE J. WARD. Hit Whilst. Treneh-Digging. Private John Ward, whose home is with his Parents at 6, Fountain-street, was wounded in the thigh by a bullet whilst trench-digging on the night of August 14th. He has crossed over to England, and is now at home on leave. Private Ward, who is twenty-one years of age, was, before the war, employed by the Long- ford Wire Company, Ltd. As a, boy, he at- tended St. Wry's Day School, Latchford. _He is at present a member of D Company, and has belonged to the Battalion for about four years. PRIVATE P. CLARE. Reported To Be Making Good Progress. • Private Peter Clam, of B" Company, whose home is with his wife at 48, Wellingtqp- street, has been Wounded in the lower part of the back, and is now in hospital at Leicester.
