PRIVATE PETER CLARE. Wounded. lie was recently reported to be making good progress, but the wound has now become rather troublesome again. Private Clare, who is 24 years of age, has served in the battalion for four years. Ile was educated at St. James' School, Latchford, and he worshipped at St. Mary's R.C. Church, Buttermarket-street. He ,is a native of War- rington, ania, before the outbreak of war, he was employed by the Castle Rubber Co., Ltd., Howley-.

DRIVER WILLIAM MASON, Wounded.

us all the time; you never know where they are going to drop. it was a shell that wounded me. It dropped and knocked the wall on top of us, and a, piece of the shell hit me. lam doing all right. It is the first bed I have slept on since I enlisted. I had almost forgotten what a bed was like." Driver Ellison, who is twenty-three years of age, was, before the outbreak of war, employed along with his father in, a greengrocery business. As a boy _be attended St. James' and lleatliside Schools. Ile enlisted in May, and on account of his having had previous experience of motor driving While in business, he, was immediately sent to the Front. 11e has three cousins serving in the Army, Encouraging Economy In Petrol. To the Town Council at their monthly meet- ing on Tuesday the Electricity and Tram- Ways Committee reported that they had decided that, in order to encourage the saving of petrol, the Corporation motor 'bus drivers should " be paid a bonus of is. per week when they averaged six miles to the gallon, and that an additidnal , bonus of is. per week be ,paid when the average is 6i miles or over." • _

employed by Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, while as a boy he attended the Ileathside School. A former Member of the St. Barnabas' Foot- ball Club, Private Irons was later employed as a fireman on the L. and N.-W. Railway. He was educated at Ileathside School, and at- tended St. Barnabas' Sunday School. A cousin and an uncle are also serving in the Army.

. FOOTBALL. TO-DAY AT WILDERSPOOL. Opening Of The Junior Football Season. To-day the season opens at Wilderspot)l with the following two matches : • SCHOOLBOYS. St. James' (last year's champions) v. The Rest. Kick-off, 3 p.m. INTERMEDIATE. St. James' v. ITeathside. Kick-off 4 p.m. "THE REST" TEAM. The following will represent The Rest" team : Back: Sinclair (Beamont): Right wing: Brady (St. Mary's). Right centre: Leah (Evelyn-street). Left centre: Mason (Beamont). Left wing: Rooney (Bolton). Scrum half: Coleman (Evelyn-street).. Stand-off half: Roberts (Haathside). Forwards: 1, Nolan (St. Mary), 2, Williams (Heathside), 3, Cole (Silver-street), 4, Hodson (Evelyn-street), 5, Acourt (Arpkey-street), 6. Deakin (Hamilton-street). Reserves.—Collin (Arpley-street), TwiRS (Arp- ley-street), Clare (Beaniont), Mannion (St. Mary's). INTERMEDIATE It I .C.BY LEACI ,E. A 'Junior And A Senior Seel ion. At the suggestion of the Warrington Club; the committee have decided to divide the League into two sections, Junior and Senior. The Junior section players will be between the ages of 13 to 16 and the Senior players 16 to 19.. All Junior players must be under 16 and all Senior players under 19. As the League matches are to begin on September 18th all Senior teams must be entered before 8 n.m. to-night (Satur- day), when a meeting will be held to arrange fixtures, &c. The Junior section teams must be entered by Thursday, September 16th. The committee hope by this new arrangement to cater for all ages— from the school age up to the militav; age (up to 19). The committee have made special inquiries with regard to the military age, which is 19. Will the members of the Senior League please make a point of attending the meeting to-night in the Pavilion? ASSOCIATION. Warrington League To Start Operations In October. The adjourned meeting of the Warrington and District Association Football League was held on Friday of last week at the Lion Hotel, Mr. H. Bate presiding. It was decided to continue the League, ac- cording to the Football Association's ruling, and several alterations to the rules were made to suit the conditions under which. the competi- tion would be played. The most important alteratinn is that tilie entrance and deposit fees. be relluced to 2s. 'd. and ss. respectively. The arrangement of fixtures was postponed until the next meeting, to be held on the 24th. Games will commence on the first Saturday in October. . , Clubs wishing to join Should apply to the Secretary, Mr. R. Brandreth, Orford, Warring- ton, or attend the meeting on tltb.. date as stated above. The following clubs have promised to play : Paddington, Hollins Green, Longford, War- rington Central, Fairfield United, Mill-lane, and Fairfield Athletic. TEAMS FOR TO-I)Ay. EMMANUEL INST!TUTE v. LOCAL AMUSEMENTS. " THE- GIRL WHO BROKE HER MOTHER'S HEART." The play of the above-indicated title, which is being presented at the Royal Court Theatre this week, is a well-written melodrama, so full of .action that at no time is there a suggestion of hanging fire. The plot is simple and rh no way extraordinary. The theme provides scope for plenty of excitement beloved of the "gods," and in the end, of course, love wins and the mother's heart is mended. The piece is capably presented. Miss Lissa Young and. Mr. Geoffrey Fulton, as the heroine and hero, respectively; Mr. David McFarlane and Miss Hero Hathaway, as the principal dispensers of villainy r Mr. J. S. Pateman, Mr. Arthur Rpdney; Miss Evelyn Vandrey and Miss N. Graham-Ed-wins all do well. "THE GIRL FROM UTAH" Due Next Monday. A great many plays have been written over the recent scare of Mormonism which has in- vaded this country during the last few years. Most of them are all of the "heavy drama" type, but it has been left to the Adelphi Theatre, London, to develop the lighter side of Mormon- ism in the latest, and incidentally greatest, suc- cess produced at that theatre, "The Girl from Utah." This, as its name implies, derives its plot from the home of the Mormons. It must not be imagined that any. lurid pictures drawn of the "goings on" of these unsavoury gentry

A BRIEF MEETING. Town Council Transacts Month's Business In Four Minutes. • What must have been one of the shortest' ' meetings of the Warrington Town Council on record was held at the Town Hall on Tuesday evening, it lasting a bare four 'minutes. The only matter concerning which there was anything approaching speechmaking was the decision of the Health Committee to erect a new pavilion at the Aiken-street Fever Hospital, with a view to providing accommodation for fifty additional beds. The Health Committee reported that they had considered the provision of additional accommodation. for fever cases. and that the Borough Surveyor had submitted to them plans of a new ward on the north side of the administrative block. The plans were approved and tenders were invited. At a sub- sequent meeting they had accepted the tender of Mr. H. Fairclough to erect the new pavilion at a cost of £920 In moving the adoption of the minutes of the committee, Alderman James Evans said that the whole subject was fairly well thrashed out by the General Purposes Committee. The minutes were then adopted. "Art For Every Day." An interesting lecture entitled "Art for eveni day" was given in the School of Art, Museum' street, last night, by Mr. C. Mawson, head master. Lantern pictures of ancient and modern wood work, plaster work, writing and illuminat- ing, ivory carving, metal work, book-binding. book illustrations, and embroidery work were shown.
